Long time stability for KAM tori of the derivative nonlinear Schrödinger equation Thanks: Corresponding author: Xiaoping Yuan. Thanks: This work was partially supported by National Natural Science Foundation of China (Grant No. 11871023 and No.12371189), Guangdong Basic and Applied Basic Research Foundation (Grant No. 2021A1515111068) and Shenzhen Science and Technology Innovation Program (Grant No. RCBS20210609103231040),.
Shengqing Hu

Abstract




Abstract

This paper is concerned with the long time stability of KAM tori for a class of derivative nonlinear Schrödinger equations subjected to periodic boundary condition.

Keywords.
Stability; Birkhoff normal form; KAM theory; Invariant tori; Hamiltonian system.

1
Introduction and main results

Let

H=H_{0}(I)+\varepsilon R(\theta,I)
be a Hamiltonian function defined on some domain in phase space
\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
endowed with the symplectic structure
dI\wedge d\theta
. Under suitable conditions, KAM theory
[
23
]
,
[
1
]
,
[
32
]
shows that there are many (in the sense of positive Lebesgue measure of the set of initial actions
I
’s) invariant tori which are today called KAM tori. On the other hand, there is such orbit
(I(t),\theta(t))
satisfying that for any
A
and
B
with
0<A<B
, there are
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
such that
|I(t_{1})|<A\ll B<I(t_{2})|
. This kind of phenomenon is founded by Arnold
[
2
]
and called Arnold diffusion. The coexistence of both stable orbits (KAM tori) and unstable orbits (Arnold diffusion) leads to quite complicated dynamical behaviors. However, all orbits are long-time-stable in Nekhoroshev’s sense
[
33
]
:

|I(t)-I(0)|\lesssim\varepsilon^{a},\ for\ |t|\lesssim\exp{\varepsilon^{-b}},

where
I(0)
is any initial action and
a,b>0
are constants, provided that
H_{0}
is steep. If
H_{0}(I)
is quasi-convex (the steepest)
[
34
]
,

a=b=\frac{1}{2n}.

(1.1)

Consequently, the theories on KAM tori, Arnold diffusion and long time stability in Nekhoroshev’s sense depict well the dynamical behaviours of nearly integrable Hamiltonian systems.

Since 1980’s, many authors tried to generalize the theories mentioned above to infinite dimensional Hamiltonian systems defined by some partial differential equations (PDEs).

KAM theory for PDEs.

Because the dimension of Hamiltonian system defined by PDEs is infinite, it is natural to seek the infinite dimensional
invariant tori (almost-periodic solutions) which are called full dimensional tori. In
[
13
]
and
[
37
]
such kind of full dimensional tori have been obtained for nonlinear wave and Schrödinger equations of spatial dimension
1
subject to Dirichlet boundary conditions. However, the decay conditions imposed on the initial sequence amplitudes are too strong to be natural. So far the problem on replacing the strong decay by a reasonable one is still open. See
[
13
]
and
[
19
]
, for example. An option is to seek invariant tori (KAM tori) of lower (finite) dimension for PDEs.

In order to discuss KAM tori of lower dimension, let us consider the Hamiltonian

H=\langle w,y\rangle+\langle Az,\bar{z}\rangle+\varepsilon R(x,y,z,\bar{z}),

(1.2)

with symplectic structure
dy\wedge dx+{\bf{i}}dz\wedge d\bar{z}
,
(x,y)\in{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
(z,\bar{z})\in\mathcal{H}_{p}\times\mathcal{H}_{p}
, where
A
is a linear operator with pure point spectra
\{\Omega_{j},j\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\}
with
d\in\mathbb{N}
being the spatial dimension of PDEs, and where
\mathcal{H}_{p}
is the usual Sobolev space with weight
p>0
. Since the existence of KAM tori depends heavily on the structure of PDEs, we need to classify PDEs in terms of the resonances of linearized operator
A
and unboundedness of nonlinear perturbation
R
. To the end, assume that the spectra of the linearized operator
A
obey

\Omega_{j}\approx|j|^{\kappa},\ \ \ \kappa\in\mathbb{R}.

Also assume the Hamiltonian vector field
X_{R}
of the nonlinear perturbation
R
maps
\mathcal{H}_{p}
to
\mathcal{H}_{p^{\prime}}
, that is,

X_{R}:\mathcal{H}_{p}\to\mathcal{H}_{p^{\prime}}.

Let
\tilde{\delta}=p-{p^{\prime}}
. We classify the vector field
X_{R}
as follows (the classification is not complete):

Case 1.

bounded perturbation (
\kappa>0,\,\tilde{\delta}\leq 0
). Here are typical examples: Case(1.1).

\tilde{\delta}=0,\kappa=2,

{\bf i}u_{t}+\Delta u+V(x)u+|u|^{2}u+\cdots=0,\ x\in\text{some compact subset of}\ \mathbb{R}^{d},\text{(NLS)}.

(1.3)

Case (1.2).
\tilde{\delta}=-1,\kappa=1,

u_{tt}-\Delta u+V(x)u+u^{3}+\cdots=0,\ x\in\text{some compact subset of}\,\,\mathbb{R}^{d},\,\text{(NLW)}.

(1.4)

Case 2.

unbounded perturbation (
\kappa>0,\,0<\tilde{\delta}<\kappa-1
). Here two typical examples are perturbed KdV (pKdV) and Kadomtsev-Petviashvili (KP):
\tilde{\delta}=1,\kappa=3
,

u_{t}+u_{xxx}+u_{x}u+\cdots=0,\;x\in\mathbb{T},\,\text{ (pKdV)}.

(1.5)

u_{t}+u_{xxx}+u_{x}u+\partial_{x}^{-1}\partial_{yy}u+\cdots=0,\,(x,y)\in\mathbb{T}^{2},\;\text{(KP)}.

(1.6)

Case 3.

unbounded critical perturbation (
\kappa>0\,\,\tilde{\delta}=\kappa-1
). Here a typical example is derivative nonlinear Schrödinger equation:
\tilde{\delta}=1,\kappa=2
,

{\bf i}u_{t}+u_{xx}+V(x)u+(|u|^{2}u)_{x}+\cdots=0,x\in\mathbb{T},\;\text{(DNLS)}.

(1.7)

Case 4.

quasi-linear perturbation (
\kappa>0,\,\tilde{\delta}=\kappa
). A typical example is KdV with quasi-linear perturbation:

u_{t}+u_{xxx}-6uu_{x}+F(x,u,u_{x},u_{xx},u_{xxx})=0,\;x\in\mathbb{T}.

(1.8)

Case 5.

fully nonlinear equation. Here is a typical example,the pure gravity water waves system (pgWW), with
\kappa=1/2
and
\tilde{\delta}=1
,

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\Phi+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\Phi|^{2}+g\eta=0&at\ y=\eta(t,x),\\
&\Delta\Phi=0&in\ \mathcal{D}_{\eta},\\
&\partial_{y}\Phi=0&at\ y=-h,\\
&\partial_{t}\eta=\partial_{y}\Phi-\partial_{x}\eta\cdot\partial_{x}\Phi&at\ y=\eta(t,x),\end{aligned}\right.

(1.9)

where
g>0
is the acceleration of gravity. The unknowns of the problem are the free surface
y=\eta(t,x)
and the velocity potential
\Phi:\mathcal{D}\ \rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, i.e. the rotational velocity field
v=\nabla_{x,y}\Phi
of the fluid.

Case 6.

\kappa\leq 0
. A typical example is the generalized Pochhammer-Chree equation (gPC):
\kappa=0
,

u_{tt}-\Delta u_{tt}-\Delta u+\Delta u^{3}+\cdots=0,\,x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}.

(1.10)

For the spatial dimension
d=1
and bounded perturbations, there have been too many results. Here are two original works
[
24
]
and
[
38
]
among them. For
d>1
or unbounded perturbations, there are relatively less results. Here are a part of representative works. See
[
24
]
,
[
26
]

[
27
]
,
[
38
]
,
[
36
]
,
[
35
]
for case (1);
[
26
]
for case (2);
[
29
]
for case (3);
[
10
]
,
[
4
]
for case (4);
[
3
]
for case (5),
[
39
]
for case (6).

Arnold diffusion or instability for PDEs.
Consider the periodic defocusing cubic nonlinear Schrödinger (NLS) equation

\displaystyle-{\bf i}{\partial_{t}}u+\Delta u={\left|u\right|^{2}}u,

\displaystyle u\left({0,x}\right)={u_{0}}\left(x\right),\,x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}=\mathbb{R}^{2}/(2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{2}.

In
[
14
]
it is proved that for given
K\gg 1
and
0<\delta\ll 1
, there exists global smooth solution
u(t,x)
to the NLS and
T>0
with

\|u(0)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{p}}\leq\delta\ {\rm and}\ \|u(T)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{p}}\geq K.

See
[
17
]
for the related works.

Long time stability for PDEs.
Firstly, look at the long time stability for equilibrium points of PDEs. For PDEs, the freedom degree
n=+\infty
. From (
1.1
), we see that Nekhoroshev’s theorem no longer valid for PDEs. Therefore, Bourgain
[
13
]
proposes to restrict the initial action
I(0)
to a sufficiently small neighborhood of some invariant set (equilibrium points and KAM tori, for example) and shorten the stable times
\mathrm{exp}(\epsilon^{-a})
to
|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-M}
for some large
M
. And in
[
13
]
it is proved that the solutions to

{\bf i}u_{t}-u_{xx}+V(x)u+\varepsilon\frac{\partial H}{{\partial\bar{u}}}=0

and

y_{tt}-y_{xx}+\rho y+F^{\prime}(y)=0

are
\varepsilon
-close to the unperturbed solution (linear flow) for times
|t|<T\thicksim\varepsilon^{-M},

subject to Dirichlet boundary conditions, with smooth initial date of size
\varepsilon
, for “typical”
V
and
\rho
, respectively. There are plenty references on this direction. Here we list part of them
[
6
,
7
,
8
,
20
,
5
,
21
,
12
,
9
,
11
,
40
,
41
]
. Especially, we mention Bambusi-Grébert’s
work
[
7
]
where the tame property of the perturbation vector field
X_{R}
is found and used to simplify the proof. Secondly, look at the long time stability of KAM tori for PDEs. It has been almost twenty years since the study of long time stability of KAM tori was proposed by Eliasson
1
1

1

L. H. Eliasson, A talk in Fudan University, 2007
and there has been few results. In
[
16
]
, the long time stability is investigated for NLS equation of space dimension
d=1
,

{\bf i}u_{t}=u_{xx}+M_{\xi}u+\varepsilon|u^{2}|u+\cdots=0,

subject to Dirichlet boundary conditions, where the potential
M_{\xi}
is a Fourier multiplier. More exactly, for a given KAM torus depending on a typical parameters
\xi
and for any
0<\delta\ll 1
, there exist constants
p
and
M
with
M\gg 1
and
p\gtrsim 8M^{4}
such that any solution
u(t,x)
with the initial data
u(0,x)

\delta
-close to the KAM torus
T_{\xi}
in Sobolev norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}_{p}}
obeys

\|u(t,x)-T_{\xi}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{p}}\leq 2\delta,\ for\ |t|\leq\delta^{-M}.

See
[
15
]
for a similar result for NLW with
d=1
. Note that
\delta
is independent of the perturbation size
\varepsilon
. For the nonlinear Schrödinger equations of space dimension
d>1
, the long time stability of KAM tori is investigated in
[
18
]
, where the perturbation does not involve the space variable
x
explicitly. See
[
31
]
and
[
22
]
for some related results.

As for the nonlinear wave equation with
d>1
and those PDEs in case (2)-(6), the long time stability of KAM tori is still open. See
Tables 1 and 2.

All the long time stability results on KAM tori mentioned above are for those PDEs of bounded nonlinear perturbation. In the present paper, we focus on the long time stability of KAM tori for nonlinear Schrödinger equations of unbounded nonlinear perturbation. More exactly, for
a class of derivative nonlinear Schrödinger equations subject to periodic boundary condition

\displaystyle{\bf i}u_{t}+u_{xx}-\Xi(x)*u+{\bf i}\epsilon(|u|^{2}u)_{x}=0,\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T},

(1.11)

where
\Xi(x)*u
denotes the convolution function between the potential
\Xi:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
and the function
u
. Moreover, the function
\Xi
is analytic and the Fourier coefficients are real, when expanding
\Xi
into Fourier series
\Xi(x)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\xi_{j}e^{{\bf i}jx}
.
Let the parameter set be

\Pi=\left\{\xi=(\xi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}:\xi_{j}\in[1,2]/|j|\right\}.

Then, we have the following main result.

Theorem 1.1
.

Consider equation (
1.11
). Given an integer
n\geq 1
and a real number
p\geq 1
. If
\epsilon
is sufficiently small, then there exists a positive measure set
{\Pi}_{\eta}\in\Pi
with

\lim_{\eta\to 0}\frac{{\rm Meas}\ \Pi_{\eta}}{{\rm Meas}\ \Pi}=1,

where
\eta
is some constant in
(0,1)
. For any
\xi\in{\Pi}_{\eta}
, the nonlinear Schrödinger equation (
1.11
) possesses a linearly stable
n
-dimensional KAM torus
\mathcal{T}_{\xi}
in Sobolev space
\mathcal{H}^{p}(\mathbb{T})
.

Moreover, for
0\leq\mathcal{M}\leq C(\epsilon)
(where
C(\epsilon)
is a constant depending on
\epsilon
and
C(\epsilon)\rightarrow\infty
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
) and
p\geq 8C_{0}(\mathcal{M}+7)^{4}+1
(where
C_{0}
is a constant depending on
n
), there exists small
\delta_{0}>0
depending on
p,\mathcal{M}
and
n
, such that for any
0<\delta<\delta_{0}
, there exists a subset
\Pi_{\acute{\eta}}\subset\Pi_{\eta}
with

\lim_{\acute{\eta}\to 0}\frac{{\rm Meas}\ \Pi_{\acute{\eta}}}{{\rm Meas}\ \Pi_{\eta}}=1,

where
\acute{\eta}
is some constant in
(0,1)
. For any
\xi\in\Pi_{\acute{\eta}}
, any solution
u(t,x)
to equation (
1.11
) with the initial datum satisfying

d_{\mathcal{H}^{p}(\mathbb{T})}(u(0,x),\mathcal{T}_{\xi}):=\inf_{w\in\mathcal{T}_{\xi}}\|u(0,x)-w\|_{\mathcal{H}^{p}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\delta,

obeys

d_{\mathcal{H}^{p/2}(\mathbb{T})}(u(t,x),\mathcal{T}_{\xi}):=\inf_{w\in\mathcal{T}_{\xi}}\|u(t,x)-w\|_{\mathcal{H}^{p/2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq 2\delta,\quad\forall\ \ |t|\leq\delta^{-\mathcal{M}/4}.

Remark 1.2
.

The measure of the parameter set
\Pi\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}
in the above theorem is in the sense of Kolmogorov.

Remark 1.3
.

Equation (
1.11
) is in case (3). Thus, the second question mark “
?
” could be replaced by check mark “
\checkmark
” in Table 1.

For the readers’ convenience, here are the outline of the proof of Theorem
1.1
, as well as some remarks:

Step 1
: Construct a KAM theorem in terms of Kolmogorov’s original idea
[
23
]
, that is, by a series of symplectic coordinate changes, to reduce Hamiltonian

H=\langle w,y\rangle+\langle Az,\bar{z}\rangle+\varepsilon R(x,y,z,\bar{z}),(x,y,z,\bar{z})\in D(s_{0},r_{0})

to

H_{\infty}=\langle\tilde{w},y\rangle+\langle\tilde{A}(x)z,\bar{z}\rangle+\varepsilon R_{\infty}(x,y,z,\bar{z}),\ (x,y,z,\bar{z})\in D\left(\frac{s_{0}}{{2}},\frac{r_{0}}{{2}}\right),

(1.12)

where
A=(\Omega_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a constant-valued diagonal operator and
\tilde{A}(x)=(\tilde{\Omega}_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
is
x
-dependent diagonal operator, and

R_{\infty}=O(|y|^{2}+|y|\|z\|_{p}+\|z\|_{p}^{3}),

(1.13)

and

D(s,r)=\{(x,y,z,\bar{z})\in(\mathbb{C}/2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{n}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\mathcal{H}_{p}\times\mathcal{H}_{p}\mid|\text{Im}x|<s,|y|<r^{2},|z|<r,|\bar{z}|<r\}.

This task is fulfilled in Sections 2 and 3.

Here are some remarks. It follows from (
1.13
) that the set
\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\{y=0\}\times\{z=\bar{z}=0\}
is an invariant torus (KAM torus) of the vector field defined by
H_{\infty}
. The key point is that the definition domain
D(\frac{s_{0}}{{2}},\frac{r_{0}}{{2}})
of
R_{\infty}
is a neighborhood of KAM torus
\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\{y=0\}\times\{z=\bar{z}=0\}
, which provides a working room for the long time stability of the KAM torus. While the definition domain of
R_{\infty}
is
D(\frac{s_{0}}{{2}},0)
in the usual KAM theorem by Arnold’s
idea
[
1
]
. In
[
29
]
, a KAM theorem has been established for Schrödinger equation (
1.11
) with the definition domain
D(0,0)
. Actually, we can expand
D(0,0)
to
D(0,\frac{r_{0}}{{2}})
in terms of Kolmogorov’s original idea
[
23
]
and that in
[
29
]
. The domain
D(0,\frac{r_{0}}{2})
can provide the linear stability of the KAM torus. See
[
42
]
. However, the expanded domain
D(0,\frac{r_{0}}{{2}})
is not yet enough to establish the long time stability of the KAM torus. While expanding
D(0,\frac{r_{0}}{{2}})
to
D(\frac{s_{0}}{2},\frac{r_{0}}{{2}})
, a basic ingredient is a suitable estimate for the so-called “small denominator equation with large variable coefficient” by
[
25
]

-{\bf i}w\cdot\partial_{x}u+(\lambda+\mu(x))u=r(x),\ |\text{Im}\,x|\leq s,

(1.14)

where
{\bf i}^{2}=-1
and
\lambda\gg 1
is constant, the coefficient
\mu(x)
depends on the angle variable
x\in\mathbb{T}
, and it is large:
\mu(x)\thicksim\lambda^{\theta}
.
When
0<\theta<1
, in
[
25
]
Kuksin gives an estimate

\mathop{\sup}\limits_{\left|{{\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits}x}\right|\leq s-\sigma}|u(x)|\leq C_{1}\exp(C_{2}C_{3}^{\frac{1}{1-\theta}})\mathop{\sup}\limits_{\left|{{\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits}x}\right|\leq s}|r(x)|,\ \forall\ 0<s\ll 1.

(1.15)

This estimate is suitable for case (2) which can be applied to the perturbed KdV equation among others.

When considering DNLS equation, we have that the vector field
X_{R}:\mathcal{H}_{p}\to\mathcal{H}_{p-1}
is in case (3): critical unbounded perturbation.
At this time,
\theta=1
. One sees that the estimate (
1.15
) is invalid for
\theta=1
.
In
[
28
]
, for
\theta=1
, Liu and Yuan gives out an estimate

\mathop{\sup}\limits_{\left|{{\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits}x}\right|\leq s-\sigma}|u(x)|\leq\sigma^{C_{4}}\exp(c\gamma s)\mathop{\sup}\limits_{\left|{{\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits}x}\right|\leq s}|r(x)|,\ \forall\ 0<s\ll 1.

(1.16)

Although Liu-Yuan’s estimate is worse than Kukuk’s one, it is enough to get
R_{\infty}=O(|y|^{2}+|y|\|z\|_{p}+\|z\|_{p}^{3}),\ (x,y,z,\bar{z})\in D(0,0))
, which guarantees the existence of the KAM tori. As mentioned above,
D(0,0)=\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\{y=0\}\times\{z=\bar{z}=0\}
is not a neighborhood of the torus
\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\{y=0\}\times\{z=\bar{z}=0\}
. This excludes the possibility of proving the long time stability of the torus. In the present paper, we improve (
1.16
) by absorbing the method by the Italian School’s works
[
3
]
,
[
4
]
,
[
10
]
, etc., which deal with cases (4) and (5). More precisely, in Section 6, we show that for
0<\theta\leq 1

\mathop{\sup}\limits_{\left|{{\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits}x}\right|\leq s-\sigma}|u(x)|\leq\sigma^{-C_{5}}\mathop{\sup}\limits_{\left|{{\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits}x}\right|\leq s}|r(x)|,\ \forall\ 0<\sigma<s.

(1.17)

Once getting this estimate, we can extend the definition domain of
R_{\infty}
to
D(s_{0}/2,r_{0}/2)
by following Kolmogorov’s iteration procedure
[
23
]
. We believe that the improved estimate (
1.17
) is of more use in the related theory on small divisors.

Step 2
: Write
z=(\acute{z},\hat{z})
,
\hat{z}=(z_{j}:|j|\leq\mathcal{N})
,
\acute{z}=z\ominus\hat{z}
, for some large
\mathcal{N}
. In Section 4,
by means of Birkhoff normal form, we reduce
H_{\infty}
to

H_{\infty}^{\ast}=\langle\tilde{w},y\rangle+\langle\tilde{A}(x)z,\bar{z}\rangle+Z(x,y,z,\bar{z})+R_{\infty}^{\ast},

where

Z(x,y,z,\bar{z})=\sum_{4\leq 2|\alpha|+|\beta|+|\mu|\leq\mathcal{M}+2,|\mu|\leq 1}Z^{\alpha\beta\mu}(x)y^{\alpha}|\acute{z}|^{2\beta}|\hat{z}|^{2\mu},

R_{\infty}^{\ast}=O(|\tilde{y}|^{\mathcal{M}+1}+\|\acute{z}\|_{p}^{3}),

and
\tilde{y}=(y^{\frac{1}{2}},z_{j},\bar{z}_{j},|j|\leq\mathcal{N})
. This Birkhoff normal form is somehow different from that of bounded Hamiltonian. See
[
16
]
for the bounded case, where the coefficients
\tilde{A}(x)
and
Z^{\alpha\beta\mu}(x)
are independent of the angle variable
x
. Since the Hamiltonian vector field is unbounded in our case, we could not find a bounded transformation to make those terms independent of
x
. This leads to the Birkhoff normal form involves angle variable
x
, which is not integrable any more. See Step 4 below.

In addition, the Liu-Yuan’s estimate (
1.16
) is still of its own use, although it is improved by (
1.17
). As the Kuksin’s estimate, (
1.17
) holds under condition
|u(x)|\leq C\lambda.
While in constructing the Birkhoff normal
H_{\infty}^{*}
, the case
|u(x)|\geq C\lambda
might appear. At this time, we have to appeal to (
1.16
) rather than (
1.17
). Recall that the definition domain of
H_{\infty}
is
D(\frac{s_{0}}{2},\frac{r_{0}}{2})
for
r_{0},s_{0}>0.
Once (
1.16
) is used, the domain
D(\frac{s_{0}}{2},\frac{r_{0}}{2})
will shrink rapidly to
D(0,0)
. Fortunately, we use (
1.16
), at most,
\mathcal{M}<\infty
times. Thus, the choice of
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}
and
\delta
in Theorem
1.1
is very delicate. See Section 4.

Step 3
: By absorbing the idea in
[
7
,
16
,
40
]
, we verify that the vector field
X_{R_{\infty}^{\ast}}
has tame property, thus,

\|X_{R_{\infty}^{\ast}}\|_{p/2}\leq\delta^{\mathcal{M}+1}+\|\acute{z}\|\lesssim\delta^{\mathcal{M}+1}+\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\lesssim\delta^{\mathcal{M}+1}

by taking
\mathcal{N}\thicksim\delta^{-(\mathcal{M}+1)}
. Remark that this step is not trivial, since
R_{\infty}^{\ast}
depends on the angle variable
x
and the vector field
X_{R^{*}_{\infty}}
is unbounded. Instead of using the tame norm in
[
16
]
, we use the sup norm of the coefficients of Hamiltonian. (See Definition
2.3
for the detail). Under this norm, by a method similar to that in
[
40
]
, we can prove the vector field of the Hamilton functions with momentum conservation has the tame property. See Lemma
5.6
. Finally, we estimate the norm of the Possion bracket in Lemmas
5.2
and
5.4
.

Step 4
: In step 2, we obtained a Birkhoff normal form
H_{\infty}^{\ast}
. Which can afford the long time stability estimate in
z
and
\bar{z}
direction. However, this is not enough for the long time stability estimate in
y
direction, since the coefficients
\tilde{A}(x)
and
Z^{\alpha\beta\mu}(x)
depend on the angle variable
x
. In this step, we will further do some transformations to make these terms independent of
x
or being small enough (
\leq\delta^{\mathcal{M}+1}
) such that the long time stability can be derived. More precisely, we reduce
H_{\infty}^{\ast}
into

H_{\infty}^{**}=N+Z+B+R_{\infty}^{**},

with

N=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\omega^{*}_{j}y_{j}+\sum_{j\geq 1}[\breve{\Omega}_{j}]|z_{j}|^{2}+\sum_{j\geq 1}(\breve{\Omega}_{j}-[\breve{\Omega}_{j}])(x)(|z_{j}|^{2}-|z_{j}(0)|^{2}),

Z=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq\mathcal{M}+2}\tilde{Z}^{\alpha}(\xi)y^{\alpha}+\sum_{|\alpha|\geq\mathcal{M}+2}\tilde{Z}^{\alpha}(x;\xi)y^{\alpha},

B=\sum_{|\beta|\geq 1}B^{\alpha\beta}(x,y;\xi)(|z|^{2}-|z(0)|^{2})^{\beta},

R_{\infty}^{**}=O(|\tilde{y}|^{\mathcal{M}+1}+\|\acute{z}\|_{p}^{3})+O(\delta^{\mathcal{M}+1}),

where the coefficients
\tilde{Z}^{\alpha\beta\mu}
is independent of
x
and
\|X_{R_{\infty}^{**}}\|_{p/2}\leq\delta^{\mathcal{M}+1}
.
In this process, we have used KAM iterative method to eliminate the function of the form

\sum_{0\leq|\alpha|\leq\mathcal{M}+1,|\beta|\geq 1}g^{\alpha\beta}(x)y^{\alpha}|z(0)|^{2\beta}.

Since
z(0)
is the initial datum, so the norm of this function will be less than
\delta^{2}
. Thus, KAM iteration works.

Finally one can easily verify that the KAM torus
\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\{y=0\}\times\{z=\bar{z}=0\}
of
H_{\infty}
is stable for
|t|\lesssim\delta^{-\mathcal{M}/2}
. Consider the Hamiltonian
H_{\infty}^{**}
. The terms
\sum_{j\geq 1}[\breve{\Omega}_{j}]|z_{j}|^{2}
and

\sum_{|\alpha|\leq\mathcal{M}+2}\tilde{Z}^{\alpha}(\xi)y^{\alpha},

are integrable terms which do not effect the stability. The terms

\copyright:=\sum_{j\geq 1}(\breve{\Omega}_{j}-[\breve{\Omega}_{j}])(x)(|z_{j}|^{2}-|z_{j}(0)|^{2})

and
B
depend on
|z|^{2}-|z(0)|^{2}
. Since we have stability estimate in
z
and
\bar{z}
direction, it means that
\sum_{j}|j|^{2p/2}||z_{j}|^{2}-|z_{j}(0)|^{2}|\leq\delta^{\mathcal{M}+1}|t|
. Substituting it into
\copyright
and
B
, we immediately obtain the long time stability estimate with time length
|t|\leq\delta^{-\mathcal{M}/2}
. See Section
4.7
.

Remark 1.4
.

Another difficulty we encountered is the measure estimate. It is under some proper non-resonant conditions that estimates (
1.16
) and (
1.17
) hold true. In other words, one needs to remove some resonant sets consisting of “bad” parameters. For example, in the process of obtaining the Birkhoff normal form, we need to eliminate the resonant set

\{\xi\in\Pi:\langle k,\omega(\xi)\rangle+\langle\acute{l},\acute{\Omega}(\xi)\rangle+\langle\hat{l},\hat{\Omega}(\xi)\rangle\,{\rm is}\,{\rm small}\},

where
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n},\acute{l}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2\mathbb{\mathcal{N}}},\hat{l}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{Z}_{*}}
and
|k|+|\acute{l}|+|\hat{l}|\neq 0,|\acute{l}|+|\hat{l}|\leq\mathcal{M}+2,|\hat{l}|\leq 2
. Note that, the normal frequencies
\acute{\Omega}(\xi)=(\Omega_{j}(\xi))_{|j|\leq\mathcal{N}}
and
\hat{\Omega}(\xi)=(\Omega_{j}(\xi))_{|j|>\mathcal{N}}
satisfy
\Omega_{j}=j^{2}+\xi_{j}+O(|j|\epsilon)
.
We hope that the Lebesgue measure of the resonant set is small. For instance, we consider the case
k=0
and

\langle\hat{l},\hat{\Omega}(\xi)\rangle:=\Omega_{i}-\Omega_{j}\approx i^{2}-j^{2}>C\max\{|i|,|j|\}.

Thus, for
|i|,|j|>|\langle\acute{l},\acute{\Omega}(\xi)\rangle|:=C_{0}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})
, the divisor
|\langle\acute{l},\acute{\Omega}(\xi)\rangle+\langle\hat{l},\hat{\Omega}(\xi)\rangle|
is not small. So we only need to discuss the case
|i|,|j|<C_{0}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})
. To obtain the estimate of Lebesgue measure, we need to verify that
\langle k,\omega(\xi)\rangle+\langle\acute{l},\acute{\Omega}(\xi)\rangle+\langle\hat{l},\hat{\Omega}(\xi)\rangle
is twisted with respect to
\xi\in\Pi
. In a standard way, we letting
|\acute{l}_{b}|=\max_{|j|\leq\mathcal{N}}\{|\acute{l}_{j}|\}
, we have

\displaystyle|\partial_{\xi_{b}}(\langle k,\breve{\omega}\rangle+\langle\acute{l},\acute{\Omega}\rangle+\langle\hat{l},\hat{\Omega}\rangle)|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle|\acute{l}_{b}||\partial_{\xi_{b}}\acute{\Omega}_{b}|-|\partial_{\xi_{b}}(\langle\acute{l},\acute{\Omega}\rangle+\langle\hat{l},\hat{\Omega}\rangle-\acute{l}_{p}\acute{\Omega}_{b})|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle|\acute{l}_{b}|(1-O(|b|\epsilon))-\left(\sum_{j\neq b}|\acute{l}_{j}|O(|j|\epsilon)+\sum_{j\geq\mathcal{N},|\hat{l}|\leq 2}|\hat{l}_{j}|O(|j|\epsilon)\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle|\acute{l}_{b}|-(\mathcal{M}+2)C_{0}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})O(\epsilon)

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle 0,

provided that
(\mathcal{M}+2)C_{0}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})O(\epsilon)<\frac{1}{2}
. However, as
\mathcal{N}
is chosen to be a very large constant (It usually depends on the constant
\delta
which is independent of
\epsilon
), this condition can not hold. Thus, in this way, we fail to verify the twist property and to obtain the measure estimate.
Fortunately, we observe that the tangent and normal frequencies has a good property:

\partial_{\xi_{a}}\omega_{i}\approx\frac{1}{|a|}O(\epsilon),\quad\partial_{\xi_{a}}\Omega_{i}\approx\delta_{ja}+\frac{|j|}{|a|}O(\epsilon),\quad 1\leq i\leq n,a,j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*},

(1.18)

where
\delta_{ja}
is the Dirac function. By this property, we can overcome the difficulty and obtain the measure estimate.
More exactly, define

j_{max}=\max\{j:\acute{l}_{j}\,{\rm or}\,\hat{l}_{j}\neq 0\},\quad j_{min}=\min\{j:\acute{l}_{j}\,{\rm or}\,\hat{l}_{j}\neq 0\}

J:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}j_{max},\quad{\rm if}\ j_{max}>-j_{min},\\
j_{min},\quad{\rm if}\ j_{max}\leq-j_{min}.\\
\end{array}\right.

Without loss of generality, we assume
\acute{l}_{J}\neq 0
. Then, from (
1.18
), we have

\displaystyle|\partial_{\xi_{J}}(\langle k,\breve{\omega}\rangle+\langle\acute{l},[\acute{\Omega}]\rangle+\langle\hat{l},[\hat{\Omega}]\rangle)|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle|\acute{l}_{J}||\partial_{\xi_{J}}[\acute{\Omega}]_{J}|-|\partial_{\xi_{J}}(\langle\acute{l},[\acute{\Omega}]\rangle+\langle\hat{l},[\hat{\Omega}]\rangle-\acute{l}_{J}[\acute{\Omega}]_{J})|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle|\breve{l}_{J}|(1-O(\epsilon))-\left(\sum_{j\neq J}|\acute{l}_{j}|\frac{|j|}{|J|}O(\epsilon)+\sum_{j\geq\mathcal{N},|\hat{l}|\leq 2}|\hat{l}_{j}|\frac{|j|}{|J|}O(\epsilon)\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle|\breve{l}_{J}|-(\mathcal{M}+2)O(\epsilon)

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle 0,

if we let
(\mathcal{M}+2)O(\epsilon)<1
. Thus, property (
1.18
) plays an important role in the measure estimate. See Section
4.6
.

Remark 1.5
.

In the present paper, the moment conservation plays an important role, as in
[
30
]
. When nonlinearity in (
1.11
) depends explicitly on
x
, the moment conservation is not valid. So the long time stability is still open for this case.

2
KAM theorem

To finish the proof of Theorem
1.1
, several abstract theorems about infinitely dimensional Hamiltonian system are given. We firstly give some notations as a preliminary.

2.1
Preliminary

2.1.1
Hamiltonian formulation of DNLS equation

Set

e_{j}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}{\bf i}}e^{{\bf i}jx},\quad j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\},

and

u=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}q_{j}e_{j}.

Then equation (
1.11
) can be written as

\dot{q}_{j}={\bf i}j\frac{\partial H}{\partial\bar{q}_{j}},\quad j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\},

with Hamiltonian

H(q,\bar{q})=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\lambda_{j}}{j}|q_{j}|^{2}+P(q,\bar{q}),

where

\lambda_{j}=j^{2}+\xi_{j},\quad j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\},

P(q,\bar{q})=\frac{\epsilon}{4}\sum_{i-j+k-l=0}P_{ijkl}q_{i}\bar{q}_{j}q_{k}\bar{q}_{l},\quad P_{ijkl}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}e_{i}e_{-j}e_{k}e_{-l}dx=\frac{1}{2\pi}.

The symplectic form is
-{\bf i}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}j^{-1}dq_{j}\wedge d\bar{q}_{j}
.

Fix
j_{1},\cdots,j_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
and let
J_{n}=\{j_{1},\cdots,j_{n}\}
. Now we choose
n
modes
e_{j_{1}},\cdots,e_{j_{n}}
as tangent direction and the remained as normal direction. Let

\tilde{q}=(q_{j_{1}},\cdots,q_{j_{n}}),\quad z=(q_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}=\mathbb{Z}\setminus(\{0\}\cup J_{n})},

be the tangent and normal variables, respectively. Then rewrite
P(q,\bar{q})
in the multiple-index as

P(q,\bar{q})=\epsilon\sum_{|\mu|+|\nu|+|\alpha|+|\beta|=4}P^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\tilde{q}^{\mu}\bar{\tilde{q}}^{\nu}z^{\alpha}\bar{z}^{\beta}.

(2.1)

Introduce action-angle variables

q_{j_{i}}=\sqrt{j_{i}(\zeta_{i}+y_{i})}e^{{\bf i}x_{i}},\quad\bar{q}_{j_{i}}=\sqrt{j_{i}(\zeta_{i}+y_{i})}e^{-{\bf i}x_{i}},\quad i\in\{1,\cdots,n\},

where each
\zeta_{i}\in[1,2]
is the initial datum and will be considered as a constant. Then symplectic structure is

\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}dy_{i}\wedge dx_{i}-\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}{\bf i}j^{-1}dz_{j}\wedge d\bar{z}_{j}.

(2.2)

Hence, Hamiltonian (
2.1
) becomes

\displaystyle P(q,\bar{q})=P(x,y,z,\bar{z})=\epsilon\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq 4}P^{\alpha\beta}(x,y)z^{\alpha}\bar{z}^{\beta},

where

P^{\alpha\beta}(x,y)=\sum_{|\mu|+|\nu|=4-|\alpha|-|\beta|}P^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}e^{{\bf i}\langle\mu-\nu,x\rangle}\sqrt{(\zeta+y)^{\mu+\nu}}\sqrt{\prod_{i=1}^{n}j_{i}^{\mu_{i}+\nu_{i}}}.

In addition, we have the momentum conservation: for the monomials in the Hamiltonian

e^{{\bf i}(k_{1}x_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}x_{n})}y_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots y_{n}^{m_{n}}\prod_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}z_{j}^{\alpha_{j}}\bar{z}_{j}^{\beta_{j}},

the following equality holds:

M(k,\alpha,\beta):=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}j_{i}+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}j(\alpha_{j}-\beta_{j})=0.

As consequence, we obtain a Hamiltonian
H(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)
of the form

H(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=N(y,z,\bar{z};\xi)+P(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi),

where

N(y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{i}(\xi)y_{i}+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}\frac{\Omega_{j}(\xi)}{j}z_{j}\bar{z}_{j},

with the tangent frequency

\omega(\xi)=(\omega_{1}(\xi),\cdots,\omega_{n}(\xi))=\left(j_{1}^{2}+\xi_{j_{1}},\cdots,j_{n}^{2}+\xi_{j_{n}}\right),

(2.3)

and the normal frequency
\left(\Omega_{j}(\xi)\right)_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}
where

(\Omega_{j}(\xi))_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}=\left(j^{2}+\xi_{j}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}},

(2.4)

and where the perturbation
P(x,y,z,\bar{z})
is independent of
\xi
.

Introduce an infinite dimensional symplectic phase space

(x,y,z,\bar{z})\in\mathcal{P}^{p}:=\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}\times l_{p}^{2}\times l_{p}^{2}.

where the space
l_{p}^{2}
is defined in (
2.5
).Then it is easy to verify that the vector field
X_{P}
of
P
obeys

X_{P}:\mathcal{P}^{p}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}^{p-1}.

where
X_{P}=(\partial_{y}P,-\partial_{x}P,{\bf i}\kappa\partial_{z}P,-{\bf i}\kappa\partial_{\bar{z}}P)
with
{\bf i}\kappa\partial_{z}P=({\bf i}j\partial_{z_{j}}P)_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}
.

2.1.2
Some notations

Let
\|\cdot\|
be an operator norm or
l^{2}
norm. The notation
|\cdot|
denotes sup norm. Let
l_{p}^{2}
be the Hilbert space of all complex sequences
z=(z_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}
with

\|z\|_{p}^{2}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}|j|^{2p}|z_{j}|^{2}<\infty.

(2.5)

Definition 2.1
.

Let

D(s)=\{x\in\mathbb{T}^{n}|\,|{\rm Im}\,x|\leq s\}.

If
W(x;\xi):D(s)\times\Pi\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is analytic in
x\in D(s)
and
C^{1}
(in Whitney’s sense) in
\xi\in\Pi
with the Fourier series

W(x;\xi)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}W(k;\xi)e^{{\bf i}\langle k,x\rangle}
,
define the norm

\|W(x;\xi)\|_{D(s)\times\Pi}=\sup_{\xi\in\Pi}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}(|W(k;\xi)|+|\partial_{\xi}W(k;\xi)|)e^{|k|s}.

Definition 2.2
.

Let

D(s,r,r)=\{(x,y)\in\mathcal{P}^{p}|\ |{\rm Im}\,x|\leq s,\,|y|\leq r^{2},\|z\|_{p}+\|\bar{z}\|_{p}\leq r\}.

Consider a function

W_{h}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n},\mu,\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{Z}_{*}}\\
2|\alpha|+|\mu+\gamma|=h\end{subarray}}W^{\alpha\mu\gamma}(x;\xi)y^{\alpha}z^{\mu}\bar{z}^{\gamma}:D(s,r,r)\times\Pi\rightarrow\mathbb{R},

which is analytic in
(x,y,z,\bar{z})\in D(s,r,r)
and
C^{1}
in
\xi\in\Pi
. Then we define the norms by

|||W_{h}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)|||_{D(s,r,r)\times\Pi}:=r^{h}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n},\mu,\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{Z}_{*}}\\
2|\alpha|+|\mu+\gamma|=h\end{subarray}}\|W^{\alpha\mu\gamma}(x;\xi)\|_{D(s)\times\Pi},

|||W_{h}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)|||^{*}_{D(s,r,r)\times\Pi}:=r^{h}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n},\mu,\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{Z}_{*}}\\
2|\alpha|+|\mu+\gamma|=h\end{subarray}}M_{\mu\gamma}\|W^{\alpha\mu\gamma}(x;\xi)\|_{D(s)\times\Pi},

where

M_{\mu\gamma}=\sup\left\{|j|\,|\,\mu_{j}\neq 0\,{\rm or}\,\gamma_{j}\neq 0\right\}.

Definition 2.3
.

Consider a Hamiltonian
W(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=\sum_{h\geq 0}W_{h}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)
is analytic in
(x,y,z,\bar{z})\in D(s,r,r)
and
C^{1}
in
\xi\in\Pi
, where

W_{h}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n},\mu,\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{Z}_{*}}\\
2|\alpha|+|\mu+\gamma|=h\end{subarray}}W^{\alpha\mu\gamma}(x;\xi)y^{\alpha}z^{\mu}\bar{z}^{\gamma}.

Then we define the norms of the function
W
by

|||W(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)|||_{D(s,r,r)\times\Pi}:=\sum_{h\geq 0}|||W_{h}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)|||_{D(s,r,r)\times\Pi},

|||W(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)|||^{*}_{D(s,r,r)\times\Pi}:=\sum_{h\geq 0}|||W_{h}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)|||^{*}_{D(s,r,r)\times\Pi}.

We say
W
belongs to
\mathcal{H}
if
|||W(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)|||_{D(s,r,r)\times\Pi}<\infty
. Similarly, we say
W
belongs to
\mathcal{H}^{*}
if
|||W(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)|||^{*}_{D(s,r,r)\times\Pi}<\infty
.

Definition 2.4
.

For a function
W(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)
is analytic in
(x,y,z,\bar{z})
on
D(s,r,r)
and
C^{0}
in
\xi\in\Pi
.
Define

|||W|||^{0}_{D(s,r,r)\times\Pi}=:\sum_{h\geq 0}|||W_{h}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)|||^{0}_{D(s,r,r)\times\Pi},

|||W|||^{0*}_{D(s,r,r)\times\Pi}=:\sum_{h\geq 0}|||W_{h}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)|||^{0*}_{D(s,r,r)\times\Pi},

with

|||W_{h}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)|||^{0}_{D(s,r,r)\times\Pi}=r^{h}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n},\mu,\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{Z}_{*}}\\
2|\alpha|+|\mu+\gamma|=h\end{subarray}}\|W^{\alpha\mu\gamma}(x;\xi)\|^{0}_{D(s)\times\Pi},

|||W_{h}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)|||^{0*}_{D(s,r,r)\times\Pi}=r^{h}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n},\mu,\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{Z}_{*}}\\
2|\alpha|+|\mu+\gamma|=h\end{subarray}}M_{\mu\gamma}\|W^{\alpha\mu\gamma}(x;\xi)\|^{0}_{D(s)\times\Pi},

where

\|W^{\alpha\mu\gamma}(x;\xi)\|^{0}_{D(s)\times\Pi}=\sup_{\xi\in\Pi}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}|W(k;\xi)|e^{|k|s}.

Definition 2.5
.

Consider a function

W(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=\sum_{\mu,\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{Z}_{*}}}W^{\mu\gamma}(x,y;\xi)z^{\mu}\bar{z}^{\gamma}:D(s,r,r)\times\Pi\rightarrow\mathbb{R}.

The corresponding vector field for the function
W
is defined by

X_{W}=(\partial_{y}W,-\partial_{x}W,{\bf i}\kappa\partial_{z}W,-{\bf i}\kappa\partial_{\bar{z}}W),

where
{\bf i}\kappa\partial_{z}W=({\bf i}j\partial_{z_{j}}W)_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}
. The norm for the vector field is defined by

\|X_{W}\|_{p,D(s,r,r)\times\Pi}=\|\partial_{y}W\|+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\|\partial_{x}W\|+\frac{1}{r}\|{\bf i}\kappa\partial_{z}W\|_{p}+\frac{1}{r}\|{\bf i}\kappa\partial_{\bar{z}}W\|_{p}.

2.2
The abstract results

Consider the nearly integrable Hamiltonian

H=N(y,z,\bar{z};\xi)+P(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi),

(2.6)

N(y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\omega_{j}(\xi)y_{j}+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}\frac{\Omega_{j}(\xi)}{j}z_{j}\bar{z}_{j},

where

\omega(\xi)=(\omega_{1}(\xi),\cdots,\omega_{n}(\xi)),\quad\left(\Omega_{j}(\xi)\right)_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}},

are called tangent and normal frequencies, respectively. Denote
\Omega(\xi)=(\Omega_{j}(\xi))_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}
, and where the function

P(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n},\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{Z}_{*}}}P^{\alpha\beta\gamma}(x;\xi)y^{\alpha}z^{\beta}\bar{z}^{\gamma}

is the perturbation. Hamiltonian (
2.6
) is equipped with the symplectic structure

\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}dy_{i}\wedge dx_{i}-{\bf i}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}j^{-1}d\bar{z}_{j}\wedge dz_{j}.

Suppose the following conditions are satisfied:

Assumption A: Frequency Asymptotics
. The tangent frequencies
\omega_{i}^{\prime}s
are of the form

\omega_{i}(\xi)=j_{i}^{2}+\xi_{j_{i}},\quad j_{i}\in J_{n}=\{j_{1},\cdots,j_{n}\},\ \ i=1,\cdots,n

and the normal frequencies
\Omega_{j}^{\prime}s
are of the form

\Omega_{j}(\xi)=j^{2}+\xi_{j},\quad j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}.

Assumption B: Momentum conservation
. The Hamiltonian
H
has the momentum conservation , i.e., for each monomial

e^{{\bf i}\langle k,x\rangle}\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}y_{j}^{\alpha_{j}}\prod_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}z_{j}^{\beta_{j}}\bar{z}_{j}^{\gamma_{j}},\quad k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n},\,\alpha_{j},\beta_{j},\gamma_{j}\in\mathbb{N},

of
H(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)
,

M(k,\beta,\gamma)=-\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}k_{i}j_{i}+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}(\beta_{j}-\gamma_{j})j=0.

Assumption C: The independence on
\xi
of P
. In the initial step, the perturbation
P
is independent of
\xi
.

Theorem 2.6

(KAM theorem or Normal form of order
2
)
.

Around KAM tori, suppose that
H=N+P

is a perturbation of the integrable Hamiltonian
N(y,z,\bar{z};\xi)
on the domain
D(s_{0},r_{0},r_{0})
with some
s_{0},r_{0}\in(0,1]
depending on parameter
\xi\in\Pi
. In addition, the tangent frequency and normal frequency satisfy the
assumptions A
,
B
and
C
. Moreover, the perturbation belongs to
\mathcal{H}
with the estimate

\epsilon:=|||P|||_{D(s_{0},r_{0},r_{0})\times\Pi}\leq\eta^{12}\varepsilon,\quad{\rm for}\,\ {\rm some}\,\ \eta\in(0,1),

where
\varepsilon>0
is a sufficiently small constant depending on
s_{0},r_{0}
and
n
. In addition, for fixed
\xi\in\Pi,P=P(x,y,z,\overline{z};\xi)
is analytic in
(x,y,z,\overline{z})\in D(s_{0},r_{0},r_{0})
, and it is real when
x,y
is real and
\overline{z}
is regarded as the complex conjugate of
z
. (We briefly call
P
is real analytic in
(x,y,z,\overline{z})\in D(s_{0},r_{0},r_{0})
.)

Then there is a subset
\Pi_{\eta}\in\Pi
with the estimate

{\rm Meas}\ \Pi_{\eta}\geq({\rm Meas}\ \Pi)(1-O(\eta)).

For each
\xi\in\Pi_{\eta}
, there is a symplectic map

\Psi:D(s_{0}/2,r_{0}/2,r_{0}/2)\rightarrow D(s_{0},r_{0},r_{0}),

such that

H\circ\Psi=\breve{N}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)+\breve{P}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi),

where

\breve{N}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\breve{\omega}_{j}(\xi)y_{j}+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}\frac{\breve{\Omega}_{j}(x;\xi)}{j}z_{j}\bar{z}_{j},

and

\breve{P}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n},\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{Z}^{*}},\\
2|\alpha|+|\beta+\gamma|\geq 3\end{subarray}}\breve{P}^{\alpha\beta\gamma}(x;\xi)y^{\alpha}z^{\beta}\bar{z}^{\gamma}.

In addition, the following estimate holds:

(1) for each
\xi\in\Pi_{\eta}
, the symplectic map
\Psi
satisfies

\|\Psi-id\|_{p,D(s_{0}/2,r_{0}/2,r_{0}/2)}\leq c\varepsilon\eta^{6},

(2.7)

where

\|\Psi-id\|_{p,D(s_{0}/2,r_{0}/2,r_{0}/2)}=\sup_{w\in D(s_{0}/2,r_{0}/2,r_{0}/2)}\|(\Psi-id)w\|_{\mathcal{P}^{p},D(s_{0}/2,r_{0}/2,r_{0}/2)},

and

\|w\|_{\mathcal{P}^{p},D(s_{0},r_{0},r_{0})}=\|w_{x}\|+\frac{1}{r_{0}^{2}}\|w_{y}\|+\frac{1}{r_{0}}\|w_{z}\|_{p}+\frac{1}{r_{0}}\|w_{\bar{z}}\|_{p},

for each
w=(w_{x},w_{y},w_{z},w_{\bar{z}})\in D(s_{0},r_{0},r_{0})
; moreover,

||D\Psi-id||_{p,D(s_{0}/2,r_{0}/2,r_{0}/2)}\leq c\varepsilon\eta^{6},

where

||D\Psi-id||_{p,D(s_{0}/2,r_{0}/2,r_{0}/2)}=\sup_{0\neq w\in D(s_{0}/2,r_{0}/2,r_{0}/2)}\frac{\|(D\Psi-Id)w\|_{\mathcal{P}^{p},D(s_{0}/2,r_{0}/2,r_{0}/2)}}{\|w\|_{\mathcal{P}^{p},D(s_{0}/2,r_{0}/2,r_{0}/2)}};

(2) the frequencies
\breve{\omega}
and
\breve{\Omega}
satisfy

\|\breve{\omega}-\omega\|_{\Pi_{\eta}}\leq c\varepsilon\eta^{6},

and

\|\breve{\Omega}-\Omega\|_{-1,D(s_{0}/2)\times\Pi_{\eta}}:=\sup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\|j^{-1}(\breve{\Omega}_{j}-\Omega_{j})\|_{D(s_{0}/2)\times\Pi_{\eta}}\leq c\varepsilon\eta^{6};

(2.8)

(3) the new Hamiltonian
\breve{P}
satisfies

|||\breve{P}|||_{D(s_{0}/2,r_{0}/2,r_{0}/2)\times\Pi_{\eta}}\leq\epsilon(1+c\eta^{6}\varepsilon).

Remark 2.7
.

From Theorem
2.6
, it is easy to see that for each
\xi\in\Pi_{\eta}
there is an analytic embedding invariant torus
\mathcal{T}^{0}_{\xi}=\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\{0\}\times\{0\}\times\{0\}
with frequency
\breve{\omega}(\xi)
for Hamiltonian
\breve{H}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\xi}=\Psi^{-1}\mathcal{T}^{0}_{\xi}
is an analytic embedding invariant torus for the original Hamiltonian
H
. In addition, the invariant torus is linearly stable. In Theorem
2.8
below, it is shown that the invariant torus is long time stable.

Theorem 2.8

(The long time stability of KAM tori)
.

Given any
0\leq\mathcal{M}\leq(2c\eta^{6}\epsilon)^{-1/2}
and
p\geq 8C_{0}(\mathcal{M}+7)^{4}+1
, there exists a small positive
\delta_{0}
depending on
s_{0},r_{0},n
and
\mathcal{M}
and a subset
\Pi_{\acute{\eta}}\subset\Pi_{\eta}
satisfying

{\rm Meas}\ \Pi_{\acute{\eta}}\geq({\rm Meas}\ \Pi_{\eta})(1-O(\acute{\eta})),

where
\acute{\eta}
is some constant in
(0,1)
. For each
\xi\in\Pi_{\acute{\eta}}
, the KAM tori
\mathcal{T}_{\xi}
is stable in long time, i.e., if
w(t)
is a solution of Hamiltonian vector field
X_{H}
with initial datum
w(0)

satisfying

d_{p}(w(0),\mathcal{T}_{\xi})\leq\delta,

then

d_{p/2}(w(t),\mathcal{T}_{\xi})\leq 2\delta,\quad\forall\ |t|\leq\delta^{-\mathcal{M}/4}.

Remark 2.9
.

In Theorem
2.6
and
2.8
, the measure is in the sense of Kolmogorov, since the parameter set
\Pi\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}
is infinite dimension. However, in the present paper we can understand it as Lebesgue measure. Indeed, if we write

\xi=(\xi^{n},\xi^{C(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})},\xi^{\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{C(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})}\times l^{2}

where
C(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})
is a constant depending on some large
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}
which are defined in Theorem
2.8
. In the proof of Theorem
2.6
and
2.8
, it is enough to treat
\xi^{n}
and
(\xi^{n},\xi^{C(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})})
as parameters, respectively. Therefore, we only need finite parameters in the whole proof of Theorem
1.1
.

3
Proof of Theorem
2.6

3.1
Derivation of homological equations

We suppose at
\nu
-th step of Newton iteration, the Hamiltonian reads

H^{\nu}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=N^{\nu}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)+P^{\nu}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi),

with

N^{\nu}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\omega^{\nu}_{j}(\xi)y_{j}+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}\frac{\Omega_{j}^{\nu}(x;\xi)}{j}z_{j}\bar{z}_{j}.

Denote
P^{\nu}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=P^{\nu,low}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)+P^{\nu,high}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)
with

P^{\nu,low}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n},\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{Z}_{*}},\\
2|\alpha|+|\beta+\gamma|\leq 2\end{subarray}}P^{\nu,\alpha\beta\gamma}(x;\xi)y^{\alpha}z^{\beta}\bar{z}^{\gamma},

(3.1)

P^{\nu,high}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n},\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{Z}_{*}},\\
2|\alpha|+|\beta+\gamma|\geq 3\end{subarray}}P^{\nu,\alpha\beta\gamma}(x;\xi)y^{\alpha}z^{\beta}\bar{z}^{\gamma}.

(3.2)

Suppose the following estimates hold

|||P^{\nu,low}|||_{D(s,r,r)\times\Pi}\leq\epsilon,\quad|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}P^{\nu,low}|||^{0}_{D(s,r,r)\times\Pi}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{|a|},

(3.3)

and

|||P^{\nu,high}|||_{D(s,r,r)\times\Pi}\leq 1,\quad|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}P^{\nu,high}|||^{0}_{D(s,r,r)\times\Pi}\leq\frac{1}{|a|},

(3.4)

where
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}
.

Write

\Omega^{\nu}(x;\xi)=[\Omega]^{\nu}(\xi)+\tilde{\Omega}^{\nu}(x;\xi),

where
[\Omega]^{\nu}
is the mean value of the variable
x
over
\mathbb{T}^{n}
.
For the tangent and normal frequencies, we suppose the following non-resonant conditions hold:

\displaystyle|\langle k,\omega^{\nu}(\xi)\rangle+\langle l,[\Omega]^{\nu}(\xi)\rangle|\geq\frac{M_{l}\eta}{(1+|k|)^{\tau}},\quad

\displaystyle|k|+|l|\neq 0,\,|l|\leq 2,

(3.5)

where
M_{l}:=\max\{|j|\,|\,|l_{j}|\neq 0\}
, and

\tilde{\Omega}^{\nu}_{j}(x;\xi)=\sum_{i=0}^{\nu}\tilde{\Omega}^{\nu,i}_{j}(x;\xi),\quad j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}

with

\|\tilde{\Omega}^{\nu,i}(x;\xi)\|_{-1,D(s_{i})\times\Pi_{i}}:=\sup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}\|j^{-1}\tilde{\Omega}^{\nu,i}_{j}(x;\xi)\|_{D(s_{i})\times\Pi_{i}}\leq\epsilon_{i}^{1-}.

(3.6)

In addition, we assume,

\left|\frac{\partial\omega^{\nu}_{j}}{\partial\xi_{a}}\right|\leq\frac{1}{|a|}\epsilon_{0},\quad\left|\frac{\partial\Omega^{\nu}_{j}}{\partial\xi_{a}}\right|\leq\delta_{ja}+\frac{|j|}{|a|}\epsilon_{0},

for
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}
.

In the following, in order to avoid a fluid of constants, we will use the notations “
\lessdot
” to denote “
<c
” with constant
c
independent of iterations. Moreover, the notations without subscript “
+
” represent the quantities at the
\nu
-th step, and those with subscript “
+
” represent the corresponding ones at
(\nu+1)
-the step.

The symplectic transformation
\Phi
is obtained as the time-
1
-map
X_{F}^{t}|_{t=1}
of a Hamiltonian vector field
X_{F}=(\partial_{y}F,-\partial_{x}F,{\bf i}\kappa\partial_{z}F,-{\bf i}\kappa\partial_{\bar{z}}F)
with
{\bf i}\kappa\partial_{z}F=({\bf i}j\partial_{z_{j}}F)_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}
, where
F(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)
is of the form:

F(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=\sum_{2|\alpha|+|\beta+\gamma|\leq 2}F^{\alpha\beta\gamma}(x;\xi)y^{\alpha}z^{\beta}\bar{z}^{\gamma}.

For simplicity, here and in the following, we omit
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n},\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{Z}_{*}}
in the summation notation. From Taylor’s formula, we have

\displaystyle H_{+}=

\displaystyle H\circ\Phi=H\circ X_{F}^{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N+\{N,F\}+\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\{\{N,F\},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}dt

\displaystyle+P^{low}+\int_{0}^{1}\{P^{low},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}dt

\displaystyle+P^{high}+\{P^{high},F\}+\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\{\{P^{high},F\},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}dt.

We need to solve the following homological equation

\{N,F\}+P^{low}+\{P^{high},F\}^{low}=\hat{N}+\hat{P}.

(3.7)

where
\{P^{hign},F\}^{low}
is defined similarly as in (
3.1
).
After we solve this homological equation, the new normal form
N_{+}
and new perturbation
P_{+}
have the following form, respectively,

\displaystyle N_{+}=

\displaystyle N+\hat{N},

\displaystyle P_{+}=

\displaystyle\hat{P}+\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\{\{N,F\},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}dt

(3.8)

\displaystyle+P^{high}+\{P^{high},F\}^{high}+\int_{0}^{1}\{P^{low},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}dt

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\{\{P^{high},F\},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}dt.

where
\{P^{high},F\}^{high}
is defined similarly as in (
3.2
).

3.2
Solving the homological equations

We firstly rewrite
P^{low}
as

P^{low}=P^{x}+P^{y}+P^{1}+P^{2},

with

\displaystyle P^{x}=

\displaystyle P^{x}(x;\xi)=\sum_{2|\alpha|+|\beta+\gamma|=0}P^{\alpha\beta\gamma}(x;\xi),

\displaystyle P^{y}=

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}P^{y_{j}}(x;\xi)y_{j}=\sum_{|\alpha|=1,|\beta+\gamma|=0}P^{\alpha\beta\gamma}(x;\xi)y^{\alpha},

\displaystyle P^{1}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}\left(P^{z_{j}}(x;\xi)z_{j}+P^{\bar{z}_{j}}(x;\xi)\bar{z}_{j}\right)=\sum_{|\alpha|=0,|\beta+\gamma|=1}P^{\alpha\beta\gamma}(x;\xi)z^{\beta}\bar{z}^{\gamma},

\displaystyle P^{2}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}\left(P^{z_{i}z_{j}}(x;\xi)z_{i}z_{j}+P^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}(x;\xi)z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}+P^{\bar{z}_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}(x;\xi)\bar{z}_{i}\bar{z}_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|=0,|\beta+\gamma|=2}P^{\alpha\beta\gamma}(x;\xi)z^{\beta}\bar{z}^{\gamma}.

The function
F
is written as the same form as
P^{low}
:

F=F^{x}+F^{y}+F^{1}+F^{2},

where
F^{x},F^{y},F^{1},F^{2}
are similar to
P^{x},P^{y},P^{1},P^{2}
. In order to write the term
\{P^{high},F\}^{low}
explicitly, we rewrite

P^{high}=\sum_{j=0}^{4}P^{(j)},

where

\displaystyle P^{(0)}=

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|=2,|\beta+\gamma|=0}P^{\alpha\beta\gamma}(x;\xi)y^{\alpha},

\displaystyle P^{(1)}=

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|=1,|\beta+\gamma|=1}P^{\alpha\beta\gamma}(x;\xi)y^{\alpha}z^{\beta}\bar{z}^{\gamma},

\displaystyle P^{(2)}=

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|=1,|\beta+\gamma|=2}P^{\alpha\beta\gamma}(x;\xi)y^{\alpha}z^{\beta}\bar{z}^{\gamma},

\displaystyle P^{(3)}=

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|=0,|\beta+\gamma|=3}P^{\alpha\beta\gamma}(x;\xi)y^{\alpha}z^{\beta}\bar{z}^{\gamma},

\displaystyle P^{(4)}=

\displaystyle\sum_{2|\alpha|+|\beta+\gamma|\geq 5\,{\rm or}\,|\beta+\gamma|\geq 4}P^{\alpha\beta\gamma}(x;\xi)y^{\alpha}z^{\beta}\bar{z}^{\gamma}.

By the definition of Poisson bracket with the symplectic structure (
2.2
), we have

\{P^{high},F\}^{low}=\{P^{high},F\}^{y}+\{P^{high},F\}^{1}+\{P^{high},F\}^{2},

where
\{P^{high},F\}^{y},\{P^{high},F\}^{1},\{P^{high},F\}^{2}
are similar to
P^{y},P^{1},P^{2}
and taken on the following explicit form

\displaystyle\{P^{high},F\}^{y}=

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}P_{y_{j}}^{(0)}F^{x}_{x_{j}}+{\bf i}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}j(P^{(1)}_{\bar{q_{j}}}F^{1}_{q_{j}}-P^{(1)}_{q_{j}}F^{1}_{\bar{q}_{j}}),\quad\{P^{high},F\}^{1}=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}P_{y_{j}}^{(1)}F^{x}_{x_{j}},

(3.9)

\displaystyle\{P^{high},F\}^{2}=

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}(P_{y_{j}}^{(1)}F^{1}_{x_{j}}+P_{y_{j}}^{(2)}F^{x}_{x_{j}})+{\bf i}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}j(P^{(3)}_{\bar{q_{j}}}F^{1}_{q_{j}}-P^{(3)}_{{q_{j}}}F^{1}_{\bar{q}_{j}}).

(3.10)

Denote
\partial_{\omega}=\omega\cdot\partial_{x}
. The homological equation (
3.7
) becomes

\displaystyle\partial_{\omega}F^{x}=P^{x}-\hat{N}^{x}-\hat{P}^{x},

(3.11)

\displaystyle\partial_{\omega}F^{y_{j}}=P^{y_{j}}+\{P^{high},F\}^{y_{j}}-\hat{N}^{y_{j}}-\hat{P}^{y_{j}},\quad 1\leq j\leq n,

(3.12)

\displaystyle(\partial_{\omega}+{\bf i}\Omega_{j})F^{z_{j}}=P^{z_{j}}+\{P^{high},F\}^{z_{j}}-\hat{P}^{z_{j}},\quad j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*},

(3.13)

\displaystyle(\partial_{\omega}-{\bf i}\Omega_{j})F^{\bar{z}_{j}}=P^{\bar{z}_{j}}+\{P^{high},F\}^{\bar{z}_{j}}-\hat{P}^{\bar{z}_{j}},\quad j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*},

(3.14)

\displaystyle(\partial_{\omega}+{\bf i}(\Omega_{i}+\Omega_{j}))F^{z_{i}z_{j}}=P^{z_{i}z_{j}}+\{P^{high},F\}^{z_{i}z_{j}}-\hat{P}^{z_{i}z_{j}},\quad i,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*},

(3.15)

\displaystyle(\partial_{\omega}-{\bf i}(\Omega_{i}+\Omega_{j}))F^{\bar{z}_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}=P^{\bar{z}_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}+\{P^{high},F\}^{\bar{z}_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}-\hat{P}^{\bar{z}_{i}\bar{z}_{j}},\quad i,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*},

(3.16)

\displaystyle(\partial_{\omega}+{\bf i}(\Omega_{i}-\Omega_{j}))F^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}=P^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}+\{P^{high},F\}^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}-\hat{P}^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}},\quad i,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*},\,\,i\neq j,

(3.17)

where

\displaystyle\hat{N}^{x}=[P^{x}],\quad\hat{N}^{y_{j}}=[P^{y_{j}}+\{P^{high},F\}^{y_{j}}],

\displaystyle\hat{P}^{x}=\partial_{\omega}(1-\Gamma_{K})G^{x},\quad\hat{P}^{y_{j}}=\partial_{\omega}(1-\Gamma_{K})G^{y_{j}},

\displaystyle\hat{P}^{z_{j}}=(\partial_{\omega}+{\bf i}\Omega_{j})(1-\Gamma_{K})G^{z_{j}},

\displaystyle\hat{P}^{z_{j}}=(\partial_{\omega}-{\bf i}\Omega_{j})(1-\Gamma_{K})G^{\bar{z}_{j}},

\displaystyle\hat{P}^{z_{i}z_{j}}=(\partial_{\omega}+{\bf i}(\Omega_{i}+\Omega_{j}))(1-\Gamma_{K})G^{z_{i}z_{j}},

\displaystyle\hat{P}^{\bar{z}_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}=(\partial_{\omega}-{\bf i}(\Omega_{i}+\Omega_{j}))(1-\Gamma_{K})G^{\bar{z}_{i}\bar{z}_{j}},

\displaystyle\hat{P}^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}=(\partial_{\omega}+{\bf i}(\Omega_{i}-\Omega_{j}))(1-\Gamma_{K})G^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}.

Here,
G
is the solution to the homological equation (
3.7
) without the term
\hat{P}
, and
\Gamma_{K}G
denotes the Fourier series of the function
G
truncated at
K
-th order, where the constant
K
will be specified later. Therefore, we obtain

\hat{P}=\{N,(1-\Gamma_{K})G\}.

The terms
P^{z_{j}\bar{z}_{j}}+\{P^{high},F\}^{z_{j}\bar{z}_{j}}
(
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}
) can not be eliminated, which can be put into the new normal form.
Finally, we have

N_{+}=N+\hat{N}=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\omega_{+j}y_{j}+\sum_{\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}\frac{\Omega_{+j}(x;\xi)}{j}z_{j}\bar{z}_{j}

with

\displaystyle\omega_{+j}(\xi)=

\displaystyle\omega_{j}+[P^{y_{j}}+\{P^{high},F\}^{y_{j}}],\quad 1\leq j\leq n,

(3.18)

\displaystyle\Omega_{+j}(x;\xi)=

\displaystyle\Omega_{j}+j\big(P^{z_{j}\bar{z}_{j}}+\{P^{high},F\}^{z_{j}\bar{z}_{j}}+\left\langle\partial_{x}\Omega_{j}/j,F^{y}\right\rangle\big),\quad j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}.

(3.19)

It means

\displaystyle\hat{N}=

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}[P^{y_{j}}+\{P^{high},F\}^{y_{j}}]y_{j}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}(P^{z_{j}\bar{z}_{j}}+\{P^{high},F\}^{z_{j}\bar{z}_{j}}+\left\langle\partial_{x}\Omega_{j}/j,F^{y}\right\rangle)z_{j}\bar{z}_{j}.

3.3
The solution of Homological equation (
3.7
)

Definition 3.1
.

We say an analytic function
f(x)=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\widehat{f}(x)e^{ik\cdot x},x\in\mathbb{T}^{n}
belongs to
\mathcal{F}(b)
where
b
is an integer, if
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
with
\widehat{f}(k)\neq 0
satisfying

\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}k_{i}j_{i}=b,\ \ j_{i}\in J_{n}.

The following lemma is useful for us to obtain the estimate of the solutions of homological equation.

Lemma 3.2
.

Consider the homological equation

\left({{\bf i}\omega\cdot{\partial_{\varphi}}+\lambda\left({1+a\left(\varphi\right)}\right)}\right)x\left(\varphi\right)=R\left(\varphi\right),\quad\varphi\in D(s_{\nu}).

Here,
(\omega,\lambda)
satisfies the Diophantine conditions

\displaystyle|\langle k,\omega\rangle|\geq\eta_{0}/|k|^{\tau},\quad\forall k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\setminus\{0\},

\displaystyle|\langle k,\omega\rangle+\lambda|\geq\eta/|k|^{\tau},\quad\forall k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n},

where
\eta_{0}
and
\eta
with
0<\eta\leq\eta_{0}\ll 1
are constants. The function

a\left(\theta\right):D(s_{\nu})\to\mathbb{C}
obeys

a\left(\theta\right)=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\nu}{{a_{j}}\left(\theta\right)},

where
{a_{j}}\left(\theta\right):D(s_{j})\to\mathbb{C}
is real analytic,
a_{j}\in\mathcal{F}(0)
and

\begin{array}[]{l}{\left\|{{a_{j}}}\right\|_{D({s_{j}})}}\leq\epsilon_{j}^{1-},\end{array}

where
\epsilon_{j}^{1-}
represents
\epsilon_{j}^{1-\rho}
for some constant
\rho
with
0<\rho\ll 1
. Moreover, the function
R(\varphi)
is analytic in
D(s_{\nu})
with
R\in\mathcal{F}(b)
. Then the homological equation has a unique solution
x=x(\varphi):D(s_{\nu})\to\mathbb{C}
which is real analytic,
x(\varphi)\in\mathcal{F}(b)
and

{\left\|x\right\|_{D(s_{\nu}-\sigma_{\nu})}}\leq\frac{c(n,\tau)}{\eta\sigma_{\nu}^{20\left({n+\tau}\right)}}{\left\|R\right\|_{D({s_{\nu}})}},

where
c(n,\tau)
is a constant depending on
n
and
\tau
.

The existence, uniqueness and related estimates of solutions of the homological equation is listed in section
6
. The proof of
x(\varphi)\in\mathcal{F}(b)
can be obtained by the uniqueness of solutions. And the proof is the same as that of Lemma 4.5 in
[
30
]
, here we omit it.

From this lemma, we know that the equations (
3.11
)-(
3.17
) have solutions obeying the following estimates

\displaystyle|||{F^{x}}|||^{*}_{D(s-\sigma,r,r)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon}{\eta^{2}\sigma^{C}},\quad|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}{F^{x}}|||^{0*}_{D(s-\sigma,r,r)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon}{|a|\eta^{2}\sigma^{C}},

(3.20)

\displaystyle|||{F^{1}}|||^{*}_{D(s-4\sigma,r-4\sigma,r-4\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon K^{n}}{\eta^{4}\sigma^{C}},\quad|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}{F^{1}}|||^{0*}_{D(s-4\sigma,r-4\sigma,r-4\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon K^{n}}{|a|\eta^{4}\sigma^{C}},

(3.21)

\displaystyle|||{F^{y}}|||^{*}_{D(s-7\sigma,r-7\sigma,r-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon K^{2n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}},\quad|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}{F^{y}}|||^{0*}_{D(s-7\sigma,r-7\sigma,r-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon K^{2n}}{|a|\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}},

(3.22)

\displaystyle|||{F^{2}}|||^{*}_{D(s-7\sigma,r-7\sigma,r-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon K^{2n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}},\quad|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}{F^{2}}|||^{0*}_{D(s-7\sigma,r-7\sigma,r-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon K^{2n}}{|a|\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}},

(3.23)

where
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}
and
C
is a constant depending on
n
and
\tau
. In addition, we have

F^{x}\in\mathcal{F}(0),\quad F^{y}_{j}\in\mathcal{F}(0),\quad 1\leq j\leq n,

F_{j}^{z}\in\mathcal{F}(j),\quad F_{j}^{\bar{z}}\in\mathcal{F}(-j),\quad j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*},

F_{ij}^{zz}\in\mathcal{F}(i+j),\quad F_{ij}^{\bar{z}\bar{z}}\in\mathcal{F}(-i-j),\quad F_{ij}^{z\bar{z}}\in\mathcal{F}(i-j),\quad i,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*},

which implies the momentum conservation for the solutions of homological equations.

These estimates can be obtained by the following order
\eqref{1}\rightarrow\eqref{3}\rightarrow\eqref{2}\rightarrow\eqref{4}
.
Here we only consider estimate (
3.23
), which is more difficult than the others. For this purpose, let’s look at (
3.17
). Let
F^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}=\Gamma_{K}G^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}
, then equation (
3.17
) becomes

\displaystyle\partial_{\omega}G^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}+{\bf i}(\Omega_{i}-\Omega_{j})G^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}=R^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}+\{P^{high},F\}^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}},\quad i,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*},\,\,i\neq j.

Applying
\partial_{\xi_{a}}
to both sides of this equation, we arrive at

\displaystyle\partial_{\omega}(\partial_{\xi_{a}}G^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}})+{\bf i}(\Omega_{i}-\Omega_{j})\partial_{\xi_{a}}G^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\xi_{a}}R^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}+\partial_{\xi_{a}}\{P^{high},F\}^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}-\partial_{\xi_{a}}\omega\cdot\partial_{x}G^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}-{\bf i}\partial_{\xi_{a}}(\Omega_{i}-\Omega_{j})G^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}},\quad i,j,a\in\mathbb{Z}_{*},\,\,i\neq j.

From (
3.4
) (
3.10
), (
3.20
), (
3.21
) and Lemma
5.2
, one has the estimate:

\displaystyle|||{\{P^{high},F\}^{2}}|||_{D(s-5\sigma,r-5\sigma,r-5\sigma)\times\Pi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|||{\{P^{high},F^{x}+F^{1}\}}|||_{D(s-5\sigma,r-5\sigma,r-5\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon K^{2n}}{\eta^{4}(r-4\sigma)^{2}\sigma^{C}}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon K^{2n}}{\eta^{4}\sigma^{C}},

(3.24)

and

\displaystyle|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}{\{P^{high},F\}^{2}}|||^{0}_{D(s-5\sigma,r-5\sigma,r-5\sigma)\times\Pi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}{\{P^{high},F^{x}+F^{1}\}}|||^{0}_{D(s-5\sigma,r-5\sigma,r-5\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon K^{2n}}{|a|\eta^{4}\sigma^{C}},

(3.25)

where we use
r-4\sigma>\sigma
and put
(r-4\sigma)^{2}
into
\sigma^{C}
.
By applying Lemma
3.2
, one immediately gets

|G^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}|_{D(s-6\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{1}{\max\{|i|,|j|\}\eta\sigma^{C}}\|R^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}+\{P^{high},F\}^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}\|_{D(s-5\sigma)\times\Pi},

and

|\partial_{\xi_{a}}G^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}|_{D(s-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{1}{\max\{|i|,|j|\}\eta\sigma^{C}}\Bigg\{\|\partial_{\xi_{a}}R^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}+\partial_{\xi_{a}}\{P^{high},F\}^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}\|_{D(s-6\sigma)\times\Pi}

+|\partial_{\xi_{a}}\omega|_{\Pi}\cdot\|\partial_{x}G^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}\|_{D(s-6\sigma)\times\Pi}+(|i|+|j|)|\partial_{\xi_{a}}\Omega|_{-1,D(s-6\sigma)\times\Pi}\cdot\|G^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}\|_{D(s-6\sigma)\times\Pi}\Bigg\}.

By Cauchy’s estimate and (
3.3
), (
3.3
), (
3.3
), it immediately implies,

\displaystyle\|G^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}\|_{D(s-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\max\{|i|,|j|\}\eta^{2}\sigma^{C}}(1+|\partial_{\xi}\omega|_{\Pi}+|\partial_{\xi}\Omega|_{-1,D(s-6\sigma)\times\Pi})

\displaystyle\cdot\|R^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}+\{P^{high},F\}^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}\|_{D(s-6\sigma)\times\Pi},

\displaystyle\lessdot

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon K^{2n}}{\max\{|i|,|j|\}\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}},

and

\|\partial_{\xi_{a}}G^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}\|_{D(s-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon K^{2n}}{|a|\max\{|i|,|j|\}\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}}.

Since
F^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}=\Gamma_{K}G^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}
, we also have the following estimate for
F
:

\|F^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}\|_{D(s-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon K^{2n}}{\max\{|i|,|j|\}\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}},

\|\partial_{\xi_{a}}F^{z_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}\|_{D(s-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon K^{2n}}{|a|\max\{|i|,|j|\}\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}}.

Similar estimates holds for
F^{z_{i}z_{j}}
and
F^{\bar{z}_{i}\bar{z}_{j}}
. Therefore, we get

\displaystyle|||{F^{2}}|||^{*}_{D(s-7\sigma,r-7\sigma,r-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon K^{2n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}},\quad|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}{F^{2}}|||^{*}_{D(s-7\sigma,r-7\sigma,r-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon K^{2n}}{|a|\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}}.

Note
\Phi=X_{F}^{1}
. Thus, by Lemma
5.7
and Lemma
A.4
in
[
35
]
, we have

||\Phi-id||_{p,D(s-8\sigma,r-8\sigma,r-8\sigma)\times\Pi}\leq\|X_{F}\|_{p,D(s-8\sigma,r-8\sigma,r-8\sigma)\times\Pi}

\lessdot\frac{1}{(r-8\sigma)^{2}\sigma^{p}}|||F|||^{*}_{D(s-7\sigma,r-7\sigma,r-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon K^{2n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}},

where we suppose
\sigma<r-8\sigma
. It implies that
\Phi
is well defined and maps the domain
D(s-10\sigma,r-10\sigma,r-10\sigma)
into
D(s-9\sigma,r-9\sigma,r-9\sigma)
. Moreover, by Cauchy’s inequality in Lemma A.3 in
[
35
]
, we obtain

||D\Phi-Id||_{D(s-10\sigma,r-10\sigma,r-10\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon K^{2n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}}.

3.4
The new normal form

From (
3.18
), we have

N_{+}=N+\hat{N}

with

\hat{N}=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\hat{\omega}_{j}(\xi)y_{j}+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}\hat{\Omega}_{j}(x;\xi)z_{j}\bar{z}_{j},

where

\displaystyle\hat{\omega}_{j}(\xi)=[P^{y_{j}}+\{P^{high},F\}^{y_{j}}],\ \ 1\leq j\leq n,

(3.26)

\displaystyle\hat{\Omega}_{j}(x;\xi)=j\big(P^{z_{j}\bar{z}_{j}}+\{P^{high},F\}^{z_{j}\bar{z}_{j}}+\langle\partial_{x}\Omega_{j}/j,F^{y}\rangle\big),\quad j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}.

(3.27)

From (
3.26
), one easily finds that
\hat{\omega}=(\hat{w}_{j}:1\leq j\leq n)
obeys

\displaystyle\|\hat{\omega}\|_{D(s-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\|P^{y_{j}}\|_{D(s)\times\Pi}+\|\{P^{high},F\}^{y_{j}}\|_{D(s-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon K^{n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}},

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\xi_{a}}\hat{\omega}\|_{D(s-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\|\partial_{\xi_{a}}P^{y_{j}}\|_{D(s)\times\Pi}+\|\partial_{\xi_{a}}\{P^{high},F\}^{y_{j}}\|_{D(s-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon K^{n}}{|a|\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}}.

In the following, we estimate
\hat{\Omega}=(\hat{\Omega}_{j}:j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*})
. From (
3.22
) and (
3.6
), one has that for
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}

||\langle\partial_{x}\Omega_{j}/j,F^{y_{j}}\rangle||_{D(s-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\leq\sigma^{-1}\|\tilde{\Omega}_{j}/j\|_{D(s-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\cdot||{F^{y_{j}}}||_{D(s-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\leq\frac{\epsilon K^{2n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}},

||\partial_{\xi_{a}}\langle\partial_{x}\Omega_{j}/j,F^{y_{j}}\rangle||_{D(s-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\leq\sigma^{-1}\|\partial_{\xi_{a}}\tilde{\Omega}_{j}/j\|_{D(s-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\cdot||{F^{y_{j}}}||_{D(s-7\sigma)\times\Pi}

+\sigma^{-1}\|\tilde{\Omega}_{j}/j\|_{D(s-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\cdot||\partial_{\xi_{a}}{F^{y_{j}}}||_{D(s-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\leq\frac{\epsilon K^{2n}}{|a|\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}}.

Together with

\|P^{z_{j}\bar{z}_{j}}\|_{D(s)\times\Pi}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{r^{2}}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{\sigma^{2}},\quad\|\partial_{\xi_{a}}P^{z_{j}\bar{z}_{j}}\|_{D(s)\times\Pi}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{|a|\sigma^{2}},

\|\{P^{high},F\}^{z_{j}\bar{z}_{j}}\|_{D(s-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\leq\frac{\epsilon K^{n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}},

and

\|\partial_{\xi_{a}}\{P^{high},F\}^{z_{j}\bar{z}_{j}}\|_{D(s-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\leq\frac{\epsilon K^{n}}{|a|\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}},

we obtain

||\hat{\Omega}||_{D(s-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon K^{2n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}},\quad||\partial_{\xi_{a}}\hat{\Omega}||_{D(s-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon K^{2n}}{|a|\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}}.

3.5
Estimate the new high order term and the perturbation

Firstly, by Lemma
5.2
, we have

\displaystyle|||\{P^{high},F\}^{high}|||_{D(s-8\sigma,r-8\sigma,r-8\sigma)\times\Pi},\quad|||\{P^{high},F\}^{low}|||_{D(s-8\sigma,r-8\sigma,r-8\sigma)\times\Pi}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|||\{P^{high},F\}|||_{D(s-8\sigma,r-8\sigma,r-8\sigma)\times\Pi}

\displaystyle\lessdot

\displaystyle\max\left\{\frac{K^{n}}{(r-7\sigma)\sigma},\frac{1}{(r-7\sigma)^{2}\sigma}\right\}|||P^{high}|||_{D(s-7\sigma,r-7\sigma,r-7\sigma)\times\Pi}

\displaystyle\cdot|||F|||^{*}_{D(s-7\sigma,r-7\sigma,r-7\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon K^{3n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}},

and

\displaystyle|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}\{P^{high},F\}^{high}|||^{0}_{D(s-8\sigma,r-8\sigma,r-8\sigma)\times\Pi},

\displaystyle\quad|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}\{P^{high},F\}^{low}|||^{0}_{D(s-8\sigma,r-8\sigma,r-8\sigma)\times\Pi}\lessdot\frac{\epsilon K^{3n}}{|a|\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}}.

Secondly, we estimate the term
\int_{0}^{1}\{P^{low},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}dt
. Actually, from Lemma
5.3
, we derive

\displaystyle|||{\int_{0}^{1}\{P^{low},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}dt}|||_{D(s-9\sigma,r-9\sigma,r-9\sigma)\times\Pi}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|||\{P^{low},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}|||_{D(s-9\sigma,r-9\sigma,r-9\sigma)\times\Pi}

\displaystyle\lessdot

\displaystyle|||\{P^{low},F\}|||_{D(s-8\sigma,r-8\sigma,r-8\sigma)\times\Pi}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon^{2}K^{3n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}},

and

\displaystyle|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}{\int_{0}^{1}\{P^{low},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}dt}|||^{0}_{D(s-9\sigma,r-9\sigma,r-9\sigma)\times\Pi}\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon^{2}K^{3n}}{|a|\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}},\ a\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}.

Then, we consider the term
\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\{\{P^{high},F\},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}dt

\displaystyle|||{\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\{\{P^{high},F\},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}dt}|||_{D(s-10\sigma,r-10\sigma,r-10\sigma)\times\Pi}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|||\{\{P^{high},F\},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}|||_{D(s-10\sigma,r-10\sigma,r-10\sigma)\times\Pi}

\displaystyle\lessdot

\displaystyle|||\{\{P^{high},F\},F\}|||_{D(s-9\sigma,r-9\sigma,r-9\sigma)\times\Pi}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\epsilon K^{3n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}}\right)^{2},

and for
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}
,

|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}{\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\{\{P^{high},F\},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}dt}|||^{0}_{D(s-10\sigma,r-10\sigma,r-10\sigma)\times\Pi}\leq\frac{1}{|a|}\left(\frac{\epsilon K^{3n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}}\right)^{2}.

Next, we are going to estimate
\hat{P}=\{N,(1-\Gamma_{K})G\}
. Since

\{N,G\}=-P^{low}-\{P^{high},F\}^{low}+\hat{N},

we have

\{[N],G\}=-P^{low}-\{P^{high},F\}^{low}+\hat{N}-\{N-[N],G\},

where
[N]
denotes the mean value of the function
N
with respect to the variable
x
over
\mathbb{T}^{n}
. Therefore,

\{[N],(1-\Gamma_{K})G\}=(1-\Gamma_{K})(-P^{low}-\{P^{high},F\}^{low}+\hat{N}-\{N-[N],G\}).

(3.28)

Let
K=\frac{1}{\sigma}|\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}|
. From (
3.28
), it is easy to obtain

|||\{[N],(1-\Gamma_{K})G\}|||_{D(s-8\sigma,r-8\sigma,r-8\sigma)\times\Pi}\leq\frac{\epsilon^{2}K^{3n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}}.

(3.29)

In addition, we have

|||\{N-[N],(1-\Gamma_{K})G\}|||_{D(s-8\sigma,r-8\sigma,r-8\sigma)\times\Pi}\leq\frac{\epsilon^{2}K^{3n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}}.

(3.30)

Combining (
3.29
) and (
3.30
), one obtains

|||\hat{P}|||_{D(s-8\sigma,r-8\sigma,r-8\sigma)\times\Pi}=|||\{N,(1-\Gamma_{K})G\}|||_{D(s-8\sigma,r-8\sigma,r-8\sigma)\times\Pi}\leq\frac{\epsilon^{2}K^{3n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}}.

Furthermore, we can get that for
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}
,

|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}\hat{P}|||^{0}_{D(s-8\sigma,r-8\sigma,r-8\sigma)\times\Pi}\leq\frac{\epsilon^{2}K^{3n}}{|a|\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}}.

Finally, we study the term
\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\{\{N,F\},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}dt
. Note

\{N,F\}=-P^{low}-\{P^{high},F\}^{low}+\hat{N}+\hat{P}.

Thus, we have that, for
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}
,

|||{\{N,F\}}|||_{D(s-8\sigma,r-8\sigma,r-8\sigma)\times\Pi}\leq\frac{\epsilon K^{3n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}},

|||\partial_{\xi_{t}}{\{N,F\}}|||^{0}_{D(s-8\sigma,r-8\sigma,r-8\sigma)\times\Pi}\leq\frac{\epsilon K^{3n}}{|a|\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}}.

It immediately follows that

\displaystyle|||{\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\{\{N,F\},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}dt}|||_{D(s-10\sigma,r-10\sigma,r-10\sigma)\times\Pi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|||\{\{N,F\},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}|||_{D(s-10\sigma,r-10\sigma,r-10\sigma)\times\Pi}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|||\{\{N,F\},F\}|||_{D(s-9\sigma,r-9\sigma,r-9\sigma)\times\Pi}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\epsilon K^{3n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}}\right)^{2},

and, for
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}
,

\displaystyle|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}{\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\{\{N,F\},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}dt}|||^{0}_{D(s-10\sigma,r-10\sigma,r-10\sigma)\times\Pi}\leq\frac{1}{|a|}\left(\frac{\epsilon K^{3n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}}\right)^{2}.

From (
3.8
), we let

\displaystyle P_{+}^{low}=

\displaystyle(\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\{\{N,F\},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}dt)^{low}

\displaystyle+\hat{P}+(\int_{0}^{1}\{P^{low},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}dt)^{low}

\displaystyle+(\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\{\{P^{high},F\},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}dt)^{low}.

and

\displaystyle P_{+}^{high}=

\displaystyle(\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\{\{N,F\},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}dt)^{high}

\displaystyle+P^{high}+\{P^{high},F\}^{high}+(\int_{0}^{1}\{P^{low},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}dt)^{high}

\displaystyle+(\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\{\{P^{high},F\},F\}\circ X_{F}^{t}dt)^{high}.

Combining the above estimates together, one gets that

\displaystyle|||{P_{+}^{low}}|||_{D(s-10\sigma,r-10\sigma,r-10\sigma)\times\Pi}\leq\left(\frac{\epsilon K^{3n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}}\right)^{2},

\displaystyle|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}{P_{+}^{low}}|||^{0}_{D(s-10\sigma,r-10\sigma,r-10\sigma)\times\Pi}\leq\frac{1}{|a|}\left(\frac{\epsilon K^{3n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}}\right)^{2},

and that, for
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}
,

\displaystyle|||{P_{+}^{high}}|||_{D(s-10\sigma,r-10\sigma,r-10\sigma)\times\Pi}\leq 1+\frac{\epsilon K^{3n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}}+\left(\frac{\epsilon K^{3n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}}\right)^{2},

\displaystyle|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}{P_{+}^{high}}|||^{0}_{D(s-10\sigma,r-10\sigma,r-10\sigma)\times\Pi}\leq\frac{1}{|a|}\left(1+\frac{\epsilon K^{3n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}}+\left(\frac{\epsilon K^{3n}}{\eta^{6}\sigma^{C}}\right)^{2}\right).

3.6
Iterative and convergence

Firstly, we define some iterative constants:

•

\eta_{m}=\eta 2^{-m},m=0,1,2,\cdots
;

•

\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon=\eta^{12}\varepsilon
,
\epsilon_{m}=\eta^{12}\varepsilon^{(4/3)^{m}},m=0,1,2,\cdots
;

•

\tau_{0}=0,\tau_{m}=(1^{-2}+\cdots+m^{-2})/2\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}j^{-2},m=1,2,\cdots
;

•

Given
0<s_{0},r_{0}\leq 1
. Let
0<\chi\leq\min\{s_{0},r_{0}\}
and
s_{m}=(1-\tau_{m})\chi,r_{m}=(1-\tau_{m})\chi
,
m=1,2\cdots
;

•

K_{m}=|\frac{1}{r_{m}-r_{m+1}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon_{m}}|
,
m=0,1,2\cdots
.

Lemma 3.3

(Iterative Lemma)
.

Consider the nearly integrable Hamiltonian

H_{m}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=N_{m}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)+P_{m}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi),

where

N_{m}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\omega_{mj}(\xi)y_{j}+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}\frac{\Omega_{mj}(x;\xi)}{j}z_{j}\bar{z}_{j},

P_{m}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=P_{m}^{low}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)+P_{m}^{high}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)
is the perturbation with

P_{m}^{low}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n},\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{Z}_{*}},\\
2|\alpha|+|\beta+\gamma|\leq 2\end{subarray}}P_{m}^{\alpha\beta\gamma}(x;\xi)y^{\alpha}z^{\beta}\bar{z}^{\gamma},

and

P_{m}^{high}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n},\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{Z}_{*}},\\
2|\alpha|+|\beta+\gamma|\geq 3\end{subarray}}P_{m}^{\alpha\beta\gamma}(x;\xi)y^{\alpha}z^{\beta}\bar{z}^{\gamma}.

Suppose the assumptions
A
and
B
in Section
2.2
are fulfilled for
\omega_{0}
and
\Omega_{0}
. In addition,
\omega_{m}
obeys

\|\omega_{m}(\xi)-\omega_{0}(\xi)\|_{\Pi_{m}}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}\epsilon_{i-1}^{2/3},\quad\|\partial_{\xi_{a}}\omega_{m}(\xi)\|_{\Pi_{m}}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\epsilon_{i-1}^{2/3}}{|a|},

and
\Omega_{m}
satifies,

\|\Omega_{m}(x;\xi)-\Omega_{0}(\xi)\|_{-1,D(s_{m})\times\Pi_{m}}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}\epsilon_{i-1}^{2/3},

\|\partial_{\xi_{a}}(\Omega_{m}(x;\xi)-\Omega_{0}(\xi))\|_{-1,D(s_{m})\times\Pi_{m}}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\epsilon_{i-1}^{2/3}}{|a|},

where
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}
.
Suppose the assumption
C
in Section
2.2
is fulfilled for the initial perturbation
P_{0}
. Furthermore, the perturbations
P_{m}
is real analytic and
P_{m}^{low}
and
P_{m}^{high}
obey the following estimates:

|||{P_{m}^{low}}|||_{D(s_{m},r_{m},r_{m})\times\Pi_{m}}\leq\epsilon_{m},\quad|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}{P_{m}^{low}}|||^{0}_{D(s_{m},r_{m},r_{m})\times\Pi_{m}}\leq\frac{\epsilon_{m}}{|a|},

and

|||{P_{m}^{high}}|||_{D(s_{m},r_{m},r_{m})\times\Pi_{m}}\leq\epsilon_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\epsilon_{i}^{2/3},

|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}{P_{m}^{high}}|||^{0}_{D(s_{m},r_{m},r_{m})\times\Pi_{m}}\leq\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{|a|}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\epsilon_{i}^{2/3}}{|a|},

where
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}
.

Write

\Omega_{m}(x;\xi)=[\Omega]_{m}(\xi)+\tilde{\Omega}_{m}(x;\xi),

where
[\Omega]
is the mean value of the variable
x
over
\mathbb{T}^{n}
. Let

\mathcal{R}_{kl}^{m}=\left\{\xi\in\Pi:|\langle k,\omega_{m}(\xi)+\langle l,[\Omega]_{m}(\xi)\rangle|\leq\frac{\eta_{m}M_{l}}{(|k|+1)^{\tau}}\right\},\quad k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n},l\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{Z}_{*}},

and let

\Pi_{m+1}=\Pi_{m}\setminus\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n},l\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{Z}_{*}}\\
|l|\leq 2,|k|+|l|\neq 0\end{subarray}}\mathcal{R}_{kl}^{m}.

Then for each
\xi\in\Pi_{m+1}
, the homological equation

\{N_{m},F_{m}\}+P_{m}^{low}+\{P_{m}^{high},F_{m}\}^{low}=\hat{N}_{m}+\hat{P}_{m},

has a solution
F_{m}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)
with the estimate

|||{F_{m}}|||^{*}_{D(s_{m+1},r_{m+1},r_{m+1})\times\Pi_{m+1}}\leq\epsilon_{m}^{2/3},

|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}{F_{m}}|||^{0*}_{D(s_{m+1},r_{m+1},r_{m+1})\times\Pi_{m+1}}\leq\frac{\epsilon_{m}^{2/3}}{|a|},

and

|||\hat{N}_{m}|||_{D(s_{m+1},r_{m+1},r_{m+1})\times\Pi_{m+1}}\leq\epsilon_{m}^{2/3},

|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}\hat{N}_{m}|||^{0}_{D(s_{m+1},r_{m+1},r_{m+1})\times\Pi_{m+1}}\leq\frac{\epsilon_{m}^{2/3}}{|a|},

where
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}
.
Moreover, under the transformation
\Psi_{m}=X_{F_{m}}^{t}|_{t=1}
, the Hamiltonian reads

H_{m+1}=H_{m}\circ\Psi_{m}=N_{m+1}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)+P_{m+1}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi),

where

N_{m+1}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=N_{m}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)+\hat{N}_{m}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)

=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\omega_{m+1j}(\xi)y_{j}+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}\frac{\Omega_{m+1j}(x;\xi)}{j}z_{j}\bar{z}_{j},

and

\displaystyle P_{m+1}=

\displaystyle\hat{P}_{m}+\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\{\{N_{m},F_{m}\},F\}\circ X_{F_{m}}^{t}dt

\displaystyle+P^{high}_{m}+\{P_{m}^{high},F_{m}\}^{high}+\int_{0}^{1}\{P_{m}^{low},F_{m}\}\circ X_{F_{m}}^{t}dt

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\{\{P_{m}^{high},F_{m}\},F_{m}\}\circ X_{F_{m}}^{t}dt.

Moreover the following estimates hold:

(1) for each
\xi\in\Pi_{m+1}
, the symplectic map
\Psi_{m}=X_{F_{m}}^{t}|_{t=1}
satisfies

\|\Psi_{m}-id\|_{p,D(s_{m+1},r_{m+1},r_{m+1})}\leq\epsilon_{m}^{2/3},

and

\|D\Psi_{m}-Id\|_{p,D(s_{m+1},r_{m+1},r_{m+1})}\leq\epsilon_{m}^{2/3};

(2) the frequencies
\omega_{m+1}(\xi)
and
\Omega_{m+1}(\xi)
satisfy, for
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}

\|\omega_{m+1}(\xi)-\omega_{0}(\xi)\|_{\Pi_{m+1}}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m+1}\epsilon_{i-1}^{2/3},\quad\|\partial_{\xi_{a}}\omega_{m+1}(\xi)\|_{\Pi_{m+1}}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m+1}\frac{\epsilon_{i-1}^{2/3}}{|a|},

and

\|\Omega_{m+1}(x;\xi)-\Omega_{0}(\xi)\|_{-1,D(s_{m+1})\times\Pi_{m+1}}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m+1}\epsilon_{i-1}^{2/3},

\|\partial_{\xi_{a}}(\Omega_{m+1}(x;\xi)-\Omega_{0}(\xi))\|_{-1,D(s_{m+1})\times\Pi_{m+1}}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m+1}\frac{\epsilon_{i-1}^{2/3}}{|a|};

(3) the perturbation
P_{m}
is real analytic and satisfies

|||{P_{m+1}^{low}}|||_{D(s_{m+1},r_{m+1},r_{m+1})\times\Pi_{m+1}}\leq\epsilon_{m+1},

|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}{P_{m+1}^{low}}|||^{0}_{D(s_{m+1},r_{m+1},r_{m+1})\times\Pi_{m+1}}\leq\frac{\epsilon_{m+1}}{|a|},

and

|||{P_{m+1}^{high}}|||_{D(s_{m+1},r_{m+1},r_{m+1})\times\Pi_{m+1}}\leq\epsilon_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{m+1}\epsilon_{i}^{2/3},

|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}{P_{m+1}^{high}}|||^{0}_{D(s_{m+1},r_{m+1},r_{m+1})\times\Pi_{m+1}}\leq\frac{1}{|a|}\left(\epsilon_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{m+1}\epsilon_{i}^{2/3}\right);

(4) the measure of the subset
\Pi_{m+1}
of
\Pi_{m}
satisfies

{\rm Meas}\ \Pi_{m+1}\geq({\rm Meas}\ \Pi_{m})(1-O(\eta_{m})).

Based on the previous iterative process, the iterative lemma follows immediately. Here we omit it. For the measure estimates, we can follow the proof of measure of
\Pi_{\acute{\eta}}
in subsection
4.6
.

3.7
Proof of Theorem
2.6

Let
\Pi_{\eta}=\bigcap_{m=0}^{\infty}\Pi_{m}
,
D(s_{0}/2,r_{0}/2,r_{0}/2)\subset\bigcap_{m=0}^{\infty}D(s_{m},r_{m},r_{m})
and
\Psi=\Pi_{m=0}^{\infty}\Psi_{m}
. By standard argument in KAM theory, we conclude that
\Psi,D\Psi
, and
H_{m}
converge uniformly on the domain
D(s_{0}/2,r_{0}/2,r_{0}/2)\times\Pi_{\eta}
. Let

\breve{H}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi):=\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}H_{m}=\breve{N}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)+\breve{P}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi),

where

\breve{N}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\breve{\omega}_{j}(\xi)y_{j}+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}}\frac{\breve{\Omega}_{j}(x;\xi)}{j}z_{j}\bar{z}_{j},

and

\breve{P}(x,y,z,\bar{z};\xi)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n},\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{Z}_{*}}\\
2|\alpha|+|\beta+\gamma|\geq 3\end{subarray}}\breve{P}^{\alpha\beta\gamma}(x,\xi)y^{\alpha}z^{\beta}\bar{z}^{\gamma}.

Moreover, by the standard KAM proof, we obtain the following estimates:

(1) for each
\xi\in\Pi_{\eta}
, the symplectic map
\Psi
satisfies

\|\Psi-id\|_{p,D(s_{0}/2,r_{0}/2,r_{0}/2)}\leq c\eta^{6}\varepsilon,

and

\|D\Psi-Id\|_{p,D(s_{0}/2,r_{0}/2,r_{0}/2)}\leq c\eta^{6}\varepsilon;

(2) the frequencies
\breve{\omega}(\xi)
and
\breve{\Omega}(\xi)
satisfy that for
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}

\|\breve{\omega}(\xi)-\omega_{0}(\xi)\|_{\Pi_{\eta}}\leq c\eta^{6}\varepsilon,\quad\|\partial_{\xi_{a}}(\breve{\omega}(\xi)-\omega_{0}(\xi))\|_{\Pi_{\eta}}\leq\frac{c\eta^{6}\varepsilon}{|a|},

(3.31)

and

\displaystyle\|\breve{\Omega}(x;\xi)-\Omega_{0}(\xi)\|_{-1,D(s_{0}/2)\times\Pi_{\eta}}\leq c\eta^{6}\varepsilon,

(3.32)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\xi_{a}}(\breve{\Omega}(x;\xi)-\Omega_{0}(\xi))\|_{-1,D(s_{0}/2)\times\Pi_{\eta}}\leq\frac{c\eta^{6}\varepsilon}{|a|}.

(3.33)

(3) the perturbation
\breve{P}
satisfies that, for
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{*}
,

|||{\breve{P}}|||_{D(s_{0}/2,r_{0}/2,r_{0}/2)\times\Pi_{\eta}}\leq\epsilon(1+c\eta^{6}\varepsilon),

(3.34)

|||\partial_{\xi_{a}}{\breve{P}}|||^{0}_{D(s_{0}/2,r_{0}/2,r_{0}/2)\times\Pi_{\eta}}\leq\frac{\epsilon(1+c\eta^{6}\varepsilon)}{|a|},

where
c
is a constant depending on
s_{0},r_{0},n
and
\tau
;

(4) the measure of the subset
\Pi_{\eta}
satisfies

{\rm Meas}\,\Pi_{\eta}\geq({\rm Meas}\ \Pi)(1-O(\eta)).

4
Long time stability theorem

Given a large number
\mathcal{N}
, split the normal frequency
\breve{\Omega}(x;\xi)
and normal variable
(z,\bar{z})
into two parts, respectively, i.e.,

\breve{\Omega}(x;\xi)=(\acute{\Omega}(x,\xi),\hat{\Omega}(x;\xi)),\quad z=(\acute{z},\hat{z}),\quad\bar{z}=(\bar{\acute{z}},\bar{\hat{z}}),

where

\acute{\Omega}(\xi)=(\breve{\Omega}_{j}(x,\xi))_{|j|\leq\mathcal{N}},\quad\acute{z}=(z_{j})_{|j|\leq\mathcal{N}},\quad\bar{\acute{z}}=(\bar{z}_{j})_{|j|\leq\mathcal{N}},

\hat{\Omega}(x;\xi)=(\breve{\Omega}_{j}(x;\xi),\cdots))_{|j|\geq\mathcal{N}+1},\quad\hat{z}=(z_{j})_{|j|\geq\mathcal{N}+1},\quad\bar{\hat{z}}=(\bar{z}_{j})_{|j|\geq\mathcal{N}+1}.

Moreover, we write

\breve{\Omega}(x;\xi)=[\breve{\Omega}](\xi)+\tilde{\breve{\Omega}}(x;\xi),

where
[\breve{\Omega}]
is the mean value of the variable
x
over
\mathbb{T}^{n}
.

Given
0<\acute{\eta}<1
and
\tau>0
. If the frequencies
\breve{\omega}
and
\breve{\Omega}(x;\xi)
satisfy the following inequality

\big|\langle k,\breve{\omega}\rangle+\langle\acute{l},[\acute{\Omega}]\rangle+\langle\hat{l},[\hat{\Omega}]\rangle\big|\geq\frac{\acute{\eta}M_{\acute{l},\hat{l}}}{4^{\mathcal{M}}(|k|+1)^{\tau}C(\mathcal{N},\acute{l})},

for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,
\acute{l}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2\mathcal{N}}
and
\hat{l}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{Z}_{*}}
with

|k|+|\acute{l}|+|\hat{l}|\neq 0,\quad|\acute{l}|+|\hat{l}|\leq\mathcal{M}+2,\quad|\hat{l}|\leq 2,

M_{\acute{l},\hat{l}}:=\max\{|j|:\acute{l}_{j}\neq 0\ {\rm or}\ \hat{l}_{j}\neq 0\},\quad C(\mathcal{N},\acute{l})=\mathcal{N}^{(|\acute{l}|+4)^{2}},

then we call the frequencies
\breve{\omega}
and
\breve{\Omega}(x;\xi)
are
(\acute{\eta},\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M})
-non-resonant.
Define the resonant sets
\mathcal{R}_{k\acute{l}\hat{l}}
by

\mathcal{R}_{k\acute{l}\hat{l}}=\left\{\xi\in\Pi_{\eta}:|\langle k,\breve{\omega}\rangle+\langle\acute{l},\acute{\Omega}\rangle+\langle\hat{l},[\hat{\Omega}]\rangle|\leq\frac{\acute{\eta}M_{\acute{l},\hat{l}}}{4^{\mathcal{M}}(|k|+1)^{\tau}C(\mathcal{N},\acute{l})}\right\}.

(4.1)

Let

\mathcal{R}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}|k|+|\acute{l}|+|\hat{l}|\neq 0\\
